#10487. Generators and Iterators in JavaScript: Lazy Data and Async Flows

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most JavaScript code loads data first and processes it afterward. That works for a list of ten items, but it falls apart when the list has a million entries, never ends, or arrives one network page at a time. Generators and iterators offer another approach: produce values only when someone asks for them. This article explains the iteration protocol, shows how generators build on it, and finishes with async generators for paginated data.

An iterator is any object with a next() method. Each call returns an object with two properties: value, the next item, and done, a boolean that becomes true when the sequence ends. An iterable is an object that hands out an iterator through a method keyed by Symbol.iterator. Arrays, strings, Maps and Sets are all iterables, which is why for...of, spread syntax and destructuring work on them.

Spread syntax calls [Symbol.iterator]() once, then calls next() until done is true. The code is correct but noisy. You manage the state yourself, and every new sequence needs the same scaffolding.

A generator function, declared with function*, returns an iterator without the manual bookkeeping. The yield keyword pauses the function and sends a value to the caller. The next call to next() resumes execution exactly where it stopped, with all local variables intact.

Calling countdown(3) does not run the body. It creates a generator object, and the body runs in slices as the consumer asks for values. Generator objects are iterable too, so spread and for...of work on them directly. The suspended state is the whole trick: the function body acts as a small state machine that the engine writes for you.

Values can also flow back in. The argument passed to next() becomes the result of the yield expression:

The first call to next() only runs the generator up to its first yield, so any argument passed to it is ignored. That detail trips up many developers.

Laziness is where generators earn their place. Array methods such as map and filter process the whole array at each step and allocate a new array each time. A generator pipeline handles one item at a time through the entire chain, which makes it possible to work with sequences that have no end:

The naturals generator never finishes, yet the pipeline completes because take stops asking after four results. No array of squares is ever built, and no number beyond 49 is computed.

There is a useful cleanup rule here. When a for...of loop exits early, through break, return or a thrown error, it calls the iterator's return() method. For a generator, that runs any finally blocks and ends the sequence. In the pipeline above, finishing take releases the generators upstream as well, so a generator that holds a file handle or a database cursor can close it inside a finally block.

Real applications spend more time waiting than computing, and async generators bring the same lazy model to asynchronous work. Declare one with async function* and consume it with for await...of. Each iteration waits for a promise to settle before the loop body runs.

A paginated API is a natural fit. Assume each response contains an items array and a nextUrl property that is null on the last page. This generator requests one page at a time and yields its items:

Once collectTitles has enough titles, the return statement ends the loop, which calls return() on the async generator. No further requests are made. Without a generator you would write a recursive function or a manual loop with its own stopping logic, and callers would have no say over the pace.

Async iteration also suits streams. In Node.js, readable streams are async iterable, so for await...of lets you process a large file chunk by chunk instead of holding all of it in memory.

Iterators define how to walk a sequence, generators make writing them painless, and async generators extend the same idea to data that arrives over time. Start small: replace one function that builds a large intermediate array with a generator, or one manual pagination loop with an async generator. The pull-based model keeps memory use predictable and gives the consumer control over how much work gets done.

#10486. The next big subscription service could be sunlight. And it might cost $5,000 an hour

A startup wants to fill the night sky with ultrapowerful beams of redirected sunlight. What could go wrong?

According to astronomers, almost everything. Reflect Orbital’s plans to launch 50,000 satellites equipped with giant mirrors is akin to “a bullet in your head,” says one astrophysicist, citing threats to both the natural world and the future of astronomy.

Selling the sun

By offering “sunlight as a service,” Reflect aims to deploy a constellation of satellites to beam down 5-kilometer-wide solar rays on demand, ranging in brightness from “noon light to moonlight.”

Reflect’s website suggests potential use cases such as powering solar panels, illuminating disaster zones and search-and-rescue missions, and lighting up worksites, cities, and events. But with a price tag of $5,000 for an hour of sunlight, the product would be prohibitively expensive for most customers—leaving critics to assume that only militaries and titans of industry are likely to make use of Reflect’s services, despite its altruistic branding.

So far, Reflect hasn’t actually launched any satellites. In August, it received approval from the Federal Communications Commission to launch a single demonstration satellite (much to the chagrin of environmental groups, which urged the FCC to reverse its decision). But moving forward, Reflect won’t need to seek approval from the U.S. government at all: It recently booked two dedicated rocket flights with Skyroot Aerospace in India, which will be liable for any unintended consequences of satellites launched from within its borders under the Outer Space Treaty.

The end of astronomy

The prospect of messing with the day-night cycle immediately raised concerns with environmentalists and astronomers alike, who are already contending with an increasingly crowded night sky, thanks to spacecraft like SpaceX’s fleet of Starlink satellites.

Gáspár Bakos, an astrophysicist at Princeton University, told Sky & Telescope that Reflect’s plans for “sunlight on demand” are “essentially ignoring all the progress we have made for the past many decades in bringing awareness to and decreasing light pollution.”

Bakos and other astronomers worry that the beams would cause light diffusion throughout the night sky, making it incredibly difficult for ground-based telescopes to view objects in space. Amateur astronomers would also be at risk of permanent eye damage if they happened to see one of Reflect’s beams through a telescope greater than 12 inches in aperture.

Lighting up dark environments at night could also pose a threat to plants and animals that depend on the day-night cycle for biological regulation. That includes humans, whose own circadian rhythms dictate sleep patterns, hormones, and digestion among other bodily functions.

Light bulb moment

In an interview with Payload, Reflect Orbital CEO Ben Nowack responded to frequent concerns about his company’s plans. If astronomers are concerned about light pollution, Reflect will be sure to avoid getting in the way of their telescopes, he reasoned.

“If there’s an astronomer or a place that doesn’t want to see light, and they have a really hard brightness requirement . . . we will make sure that we don’t serve customers close [by],” Nowack said.

As for anxieties around the impact of life on Earth, Nowack chalks them up to a general fear of new technology. “Similar concerns and fears were brought up when light bulbs were introduced to the world, and people were worried about circadian rhythm and, like, is this godly?” he said. 

Never mind the fact that Reflect’s beams of sunlight would be just a touch brighter than light bulbs, casting light for up to 30 kilometers from their targets. Each satellite would also appear in the sky as a point source four magnitudes brighter than the moon, according to a report by Bakos and his colleagues.

“This is not a ‘concern’—this is like a bullet in your head,” Bakos said. “It’s the end of ground-based astronomy.”

#10485. This is your rare chance to get a Garmin smartwatch for under $170

The Garmin Vivoactive 5 is down to its lowest price of all time in all four colors for a limited time, making this the least expensive Garmin you can buy today.

Garmin smartwatches are known for a lot of things. Extremely long battery life, epic health stat tracking, universal phone compatibility, and lots of training plans and tracked exercises. But they’re not known for being low-priced or particularly affordable. That is, until today’s Garmin Vivoactive 5 deal came along.

At just $169.99, this Garmin Vivoactive 5 deal brings the watch down to the lowest price it’s ever been. That’s a particularly excellent price since Garmin isn’t pushy with subscription costs the way some other brands can be. Buy your Garmin and use it. That’s the only real cost unless you need something very specific, and Garmin has several subscriptions for somewhat random things.

Garmin’s Vivoactive lineup is made for casual athletes who find themselves running every so often, go to the gym regularly (but aren’t trying to beat Olympic records), and just find workouts to be a healthy part of normal life. Garmin came out with a Vivoactive 6 earlier this year, which retails for $299, and only offers a few advantages that you might care about. And yes, it even supports proper messaging, images, and replies on Android!

Both the Vivoactive 5 and 6 sport the same 11-day battery life, an AMOLED display with 360 x 360 resolution, 5ATM water depth rating, and 23g weight (without the band). The Vivoactive 6 boosts the storage to 8Gb (from 4GB on the Vivoactive 5) and has a new UI, supports Garmin’s Smart Wake feature, and also includes Garmin’s PacePro feature.

Smart Wake is cool, but only people who wear their watch 24/7 will benefit from it. Likewise, running fans may enjoy PacePro, which gives pace guidance throughout a run. I never use offline song downloads for runs, but double the storage could be worth it if you do. If those features aren’t a big deal, this Garmin Vivoactive 5 deal is the best price you’re going to find on a Garmin smartwatch.

#10483. Taco Bell says it has a ‘magic formula’ for bringing customers back. Here’s what it is

Yum Brands, the parent company of Taco Bell, KFC, and The Habit Burger & Grill, reported higher-than-expected third-quarter earnings on Tuesday, driven in part by KFC’s global expansion and higher revenue from its franchises.

However, an outbreak of cyclosporiasis linked to tainted shredded lettuce at Taco Bell, which Yum considers its “growth engine,” affected the chain’s same-store sales, which dropped 4% in the U.S. for the quarter.

Despite that, it appears consumers have not lost their appetite for Taco Bell. On Tuesday’s earnings call, Yum CFO Ranjith Roy said that looking ahead to the fourth quarter, same-store sales are up about 1%—and up 3% over the last four weeks—compared with the same period last year.

Yum attributed the fast bounce back to a “magic formula” that included Tuesday weekly deals for loyalty members and limited-time offers.

“Since the end of September, Taco Bell U.S. has been outpacing the [quick-service restaurant] industry on same-store sales growth,” Yum Brands CEO Chris Turner said in an earnings statement. “Taco Bell will continue building on this momentum, with differentiated value and innovation that resonate across all consumer groups. . . . [We] remain confident in our ability to deliver consistent earnings growth and create long-term shareholder value.”

Shares of Yum Brands Inc. (NYSE: YUM) were down 0.2% in Wednesday afternoon trading.

For the quarter ending September 30, Yum Brands’ reported revenue increased 3.8%, coming in at $2.05 billion, with earnings per share (EPS) of $1.56 adjusted, exceeding the Zacks Consensus Estimate of $1.47. Net income rose to $1.62 billion, up from $397 million at the same time last year.

The company said the gains excluded the company’s recently completed divestment from Pizza Hut, which was acquired by LongRange Capital and Yum China Holdings Inc.

#10481. AI chip smugglers exploit trade control loophole to bring banned HBM from South Korea into China

Is it smuggling if it's technically legal?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A memory smuggler has detailed how he gets export-controlled HBM chips from South Korea into China using a loophole. According to The Information, the smuggler owns a chip design firm that build cheap, low-performing processors that fall under the U.S. and South Korea’s export controls. They are then paired with expensive HBM from Samsung and SK hynix, that, on their own, are banned for export to China. But because export control rules only look at the performance of the server processor and not at the other components attached to it, this allows the HBM chips attached inside them to slip under the radar and make their way into China.

It’s not a risk-free operation, though. Once the mediocre processors with high-end memory chips land in Hong Kong and are smuggled across the border into Shenzhen in the luggage of commuters that cross between the two regions daily, they end up in workshops where technicians heat up the boards to soften the solder, gingerly lift the crucial memory chips, and discard the rest of the components. Despite having a high failure rate, the high price of HBM chips makes the procedure quite lucrative.

A Shenzhen-based chip broker has been making big money matching up suppliers such as the smuggler and companies that are desperate for HBM. “A year ago, a single HBM3E unit changed hands in China for several hundred dollars,” he tells the publication. “Today it’s about $4,000.” With an AI chip requiring six to eight high-end memory chips, that means the broker is raking in $24,000 to $32,000 for every AI GPU.

Chinese AI labs face this exact memory chip bottleneck when acquiring AI GPUs. While Chinese memory manufacturer CXMT has already begun risk production of HBM3E and is expected to mass produce them by the end of this year, it’s still reportedly suffering from low yield, which makes their chips expensive to produce and procure. We see this with Huawei’s Ascend 950DT, its current top-of-the-line processor that goes for about $37,000 — it offers similar pricing to Nvidia’s B200 AI GPU but only delivers half the performance of the previous-generation H100.

Huawei has reportedly said that its next-generation flagship AI processor, the 960DT, would deliver a similar performance as the H100. However, since the company cannot purchase HBM from Samsung and SK hynix, and Chinese memory manufacturers do not have the capacity to supply all its chip demands, Huawei told customers that they have to find the HBM that these servers need themselves. This would further fuel the demand for these chips, increase the price and make it more lucrative for smugglers to set up similar operations.

But given that this method of moving HBM chips into China is becoming more popular, the smuggler fears that South Korean and U.S. authorities would clamp down on their operations soon. Washington has been going down hard on activities like this, and we’ve seen high-profile arrests including some Super Micro executives and the CEO of a California-based tech company. Other countries like Taiwan and Malaysia have been ramping up their anti-smuggling activities, too, so it’s probably just a matter of time before Seoul follows suit.

#10480. Hinge is making a surprising change to its subscriptions. Here’s who stands to benefit

Hinge users may soon have access to a more affordable subscription tier. The app’s parent company, Match Group, announced on November 2 that it’s testing a new feature geared toward women.

During its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, Match Group said the new tier will give users more control through additional filters and custom location boundaries.

“It’s designed for women in particular, but also for price-sensitive users,” Match Group CFO Steve Bailey said during the call. 

Hinge currently offers three tiers: a free version with limits on filters and daily likes; and the more customizable Hinge+ and HingeX, priced at $89.99 and $99.99 for three months, respectively. 

Tinder has similar subscription tiers, with Tinder Plus and Tinder Gold offering perks like unlimited swipes and top picks for around $25 and $35 per month, respectively.

“Hinge is one of the highest-priced products we have. The revenue per payer, for example, at Hinge is almost twice that of Tinder,” Bailey said. “It also has one of the lower payer penetrations. Its payer penetration is only slightly more than half that of Tinder.”

The company did not respond to Fast Company’s request for additional details about the new subscription tier. 

The lower-priced offering comes as consumers grow increasingly frustrated with the dating ecosystem. In a February 2026 Harris Poll, 72% of Americans said romance feels more like a transaction due to dating apps, while 77% said dating has become a marketplace.

Despite those sentiments, Hinge’s monthly active user numbers are up 12% year over year.

While Tinder is still bigger, with around 8.9 million paid users, its base is shrinking, with its paid users down 4% from last year. Tinder has since leaned on a different strategy to keep users engaged, including facilitating in-person experiences to shift the focus away from swiping.

Match Group owns a number of major dating platforms in addition to Match, Tinder, and Hinge, including OkCupid and Plenty of Fish.

#10477. The 2026 midterm elections could change everything for Trump. Here’s what’s at stake

The midterm elections have the potential to upend everything from politics to business to the stock market. But the person most impacted by Tuesday’s results could be President Donald Trump, and that, in effect, could determine the course of his final two years in office.

On the political front, the battle to control Congress is key, with a number of seats in play, including 435 in the House of Representatives and 35 in the Senate.

Should the Democrats gain control of the House (and, perhaps, even the Senate), the voting public will have broken the Trump administration and Republicans’ stronghold over Congress.

“The midterms matter in general because they determine whether there is alignment between the executive and the legislative branches,” Harvard Business School professor Vincent Pons said in an interview earlier this year. “Oftentimes, voters vote against the party of the president. Some voters have a preference for divided government, to have checks and balances. If the Republican party was to lose the midterms, either the House or both the House and the Senate, it could have dramatic consequences in terms of circumventing the power of [the administration].”

A Democratic upset could check Trump’s power, paving the way for a lame-duck presidency for the remainder of his term. A blue sweep could also give Dems the votes they need to investigate the president’s finances, his administration’s immigration policies, and bring another impeachment.

In August, Michigan Democrat and Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed told CNN, “The first thing we’re going to do when [the Democrats] win this Senate seat? Hold Trump accountable. Then, on to the real work—taking on big corporations, taxing billionaires their wealth, and guaranteeing healthcare through Medicare for All.”

Back in January, Democratic Representative Micah Lasher of New York posted a road map for Congressional oversight should the Democrats regain control of the House: “Check the Trump administration’s lawlessness by making clear that illegal activity and corruption will be investigated, and by spurring criminal prosecutions or impeachments; focus the American public on the illegal and corrupt activity that gets lost in the daily barrage of news and distraction out of the White House; and shape the narrative of the 2028 elections and help Democrats win back the White House and grow our majority.”

In California, Democratic Representative Robert Garcia said he plans to look into the White House’s immigration policies, and “demand accountability” for the tactics used by Immigration and Customs Enforcement—the fallout of which would likely influence the 2028 presidential election.

In July, House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, a New York Democrat, said, “When you put us back in charge of the United States House of Representatives, you’ll get a Congress that speaks up for everyday Americans and a Congress that stands up to an out-of-control president.” Jeffries emphasized that to beat Republicans, Democrats were focused on healthcare costs, bringing down the high cost of living, and ending the war in Iran.

A divided Congress, with Republicans maintaining their majority in at least one branch, could result in major political gridlock for the remainder of Trump’s term, which could have major repercussions for business.

If Republicans can hold on to their seats, that could empower Trump to accelerate his domestic agenda and policies on key issues like defense and AI—with those government-connected sectors seeing continued growth, and banks and cryptocurrencies retaining lighter regulation.

#10475. How to Source Passive Candidates with AI in 2026

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Passive candidates answer when a message is specific, short and clearly about them. This is a nine-step playbook, and each step shows how to do it in Pin. The method works in any tool. For a shorter version, see Pin's 7-step guide to sourcing passive candidates.

For recruiters sourcing passive talent at scale, Pin covers the workflow end to end. Pin says it searches 850M+ profiles and ranks them against your brief, and the recruiter makes every call.

A passive candidate is a working professional who isn't applying for jobs but might say yes to the right conversation.

Gallup's fourth-quarter 2025 panel sizes the pool. It found that 51% of U.S. workers are actively looking (11%) or watching for opportunities (40%).

The practical reading: only 11% of workers are actively looking, so most of the market isn't on a job board, and about half is paying attention.

Skills-based pools are larger. That is a modeled estimate, with "eligible" meaning at least half of the target job's top skills.

Senior data engineer who has built a warehouse from scratch at a Series B to D fintech. Strong dbt and Airflow. Remote in US time zones. Avoid consultancies.

The string misses anyone titled "Software Engineer, Data Platform" who has done the same work. Skills-based matching reads the work, so title mismatches stop hiding good people. Filters can be written in natural language, and advanced filters stay under your control.

Recruiters add the LinkedIn touch for the candidates they want most, so part of that gap reflects prioritization.

#10472. iPad Mini review: A pricey delight

For a device with such a straightforward name, the iPad Mini has had many lives over its 14 years. Yes, it’s always been a small iPad. But at various times, it’s also been a cheap iPad with a lesser display, an iPad as technologically sophisticated as any you could buy, and a midrange iPad.

Last week, Apple announced a new iPad—one of the remarkably large number of products the company introduced during what may have been its most productive October ever. It remains a small iPad. But the new version replaces the LCD screen of past versions with OLED, a technology previously available only in the iPad Pro. That’s one of several upgrades that take the new Mini to a tier of its own—not quite on the level of the iPad Pro, but in some respects above the iPad Air models the previous Mini closely resembled.

The new iPad Mini also costs $100 more than its predecessor. That version went up $100 itself in June, when Apple raised many of its prices in reaction to the AI-induced RAM shortage. Now the Mini starts at an imposing $699 for the Wi-Fi version with 128 GB of storage, a 40% increase in a little over four months, and just $50 less than the cheapest iPad Air. (The basic, entry-level iPad also went up $100, but I still consider it a bargain at $449.)

Even if the iPad Mini is no longer an impulse item for most of us, it remains the present-day iPad that reminds me most of Steve Jobs’s keynote introduction of the original 2010 iPad. In it, he outlined the need for the device and did much of his demo from the comfort of a cushy chair. That niche—bigger than a phone, more intimate than a laptop—is alive, well, and still evolving in the new Mini.

The Mini’s roomy screen lets it do many things well that an iPhone can’t, unless it’s an iPhone Duo. Unlike its larger iPad siblings, however, it’s not trying to encroach on the traditional territory of a MacBook or other PCs. It’s a tablet in the category’s purest sense. (I am aware that third-party manufacturers have long made keyboard cases for iPad Minis and will surely offer ones for this model—I just can’t envision even that setup replacing anyone’s laptop.)

Since the beginning, the Mini has been all about size, weight, and the pleasure of spending time with a device that you can hold for extended periods—in one hand, even—without it seeming to grow mysteriously heavier over time. It’s also been the easiest iPad to grab and take almost anywhere, maybe in a jacket pocket.

After all these years, Apple is still finding ways to wring heft out of the new Mini’s design. Compared to the previous version, the most striking improvements are the 16% reduction in thickness and (for the Wi-Fi model) 13.8% reduction in weight. But Apple also shrank the display’s border by 25%, which allowed it to increase the screen size from 8.3 inches to 8.5 inches, even though the new model is slightly narrower. It’s about 45% lighter than the 11-inch iPad Air and 59% lighter than the 13-inch Air.

Consequently, this tablet is a delight for tasks where more display real estate than a (non-folding) phone is desirable, but 11 or 13 inches may be overkill—long-form reading on the web or in an e-reader app, gaming, viewing and wrangling photos, lightweight productivity such as email triage. It’s also an extremely pleasant way to watch video, assuming you’re not trying to re-create the experience of watching a blockbuster movie in the theater. And its support for Apple Pencil Pro makes it a beguiling digital sketchbook when you’re out and about and lugging a larger iPad would feel like a burden.

As for the new OLED screen, it delivers on the technology’s promise by providing deep blacks, rich colors, and peak brightness of 1,200 nits, compared to 500 nits for the previous model. Some people—such as Multicore’s Sam Byford—regret that the display doesn’t include Apple’s ProMotion technology, which uses a variable refresh rate to make scrolling and input smoother and more responsive. I don’t find its omission glaring. (The 256 GB and 512 GB versions do offer Apple’s glare-reducing nano-texture screen—previously exclusive to the iPad Pro—as a $100 option.)

The new Mini is the first iPad to incorporate an audio system called Acoustic Display, which moves the speakers behind the screen. The resulting output sounds crisper to me than what I’m used to on my 13-inch iPad Pro. Just as notable, the new location allowed Apple to seal up the tablet, making it the first iPad ever to achieve an IP67 rating for water and dust resistance. It should be able to withstand being immersed in 3 feet of water for 30 minutes—a feature that makes sense considering it’s the iPad you’re most likely to take to the beach or pool, or use in a bathtub.

Another change brings the iPad Mini in line with other current iPads: Its front-facing camera is on the tablet’s landscape edge rather than the portrait one. If you’re using a larger iPad in a keyboard case, that’s unquestionably the proper spot, since it centers the camera above the screen rather than putting it on the side, which can leave you looking shifty-eyed on video calls.

I have always used iPad Minis mostly in portrait mode, so I’m not as sure the camera relocation is an improvement in this case. But it’s not an issue as long as you remember to rotate the tablet into landscape mode for FaceTime, Zoom, and the like.

Despite the new Mini’s more upscale feel, Apple has stuck with a Touch ID sensor rather than equipping it with Face ID, which remains exclusive to iPhones and the iPad Pro. It’s built into the power button and, in my experience, works close to 100% of the time. But it requires more thought to use than Face ID, especially since the sensor’s location depends on how you’re holding the Mini. It’s the one area where using the Mini feels like less than a first-class experience.

Then there’s the Mini’s processor, which I haven’t mentioned until now because so many of the tasks this tablet is well suited to don’t cry out for raw performance. (Fancy 3D games and on-device AI are exceptions.)

Apple didn’t skimp: It chose the A20 Pro chip, a processor that is itself new, having debuted in September with the iPhone 18 Pro. The company touts benchmark numbers such as up to 60% faster CPU performance and up to 270% faster GPU performance than the previous Mini. That version used the A17 Pro, a chip that wasn’t new even when that tablet was released two years ago.

Even if you don’t care how much faster a new iPad is than its predecessor, the fact that the tablet has Apple’s latest chip is a boon. It will extend the tablet’s useful life before it struggles to run new apps or is no longer supported by iPadOS updates. That may be more important for iPads than other devices: Anecdotally, at least, people tend to hold on to them for years.

One other thing about the new iPad Mini is worth mentioning—not because it’s changed, but because it hasn’t. The tablet runs iPadOS 27, the latest version of the iPad’s operating system. That sports the same multitasking interface introduced in iPadOS 26—a Mac-esque world with floating windows, menus, and traffic-light-like buttons for expanding, minimizing, and closing windows. Even on the iPad Pro I use as a work computer for hours a day, I am not enthralled with the clutter and complexity it brings. On a Mini, actually trying to use these features feels like suffocating overkill.

When setting up a new iPad Mini, you can avoid this experience altogether by choosing the “Full Screen Apps” option. That limits you to one on-screen app at a time, just like the iPads of yore. That, I suspect, will be plenty for a high percentage of users. If you expect to be one of them, you can stop reading now.

Still, there is value in letting a Mini-size device do a certain amount of multitasking, particularly when you’re holding it in landscape orientation and performing tasks that involve multiple apps. As it happens, Apple just took a fresh shot at creating an interface tuned to this scenario.

That would be the one offered by the iPhone Duo, whose 7.6-inch interior display is close enough in size to the iPad Mini that many people instinctively talk about the Duo as if it were a Mini you could fold up and put in your pocket. It offers far fewer options than iPad multitasking, but provides a more intuitive, foolproof way to perform the one form of multitasking that’s most useful on a small tablet: putting two apps side by side without letting them overlap or float.

Now, the iPhone Duo is definitely an iPhone. It’s obvious why it doesn’t borrow iPadOS 26/27-style multitasking, which would be bizarre on a device so many people use for quick hits of information when they’re walking around. The fact that the Duo can’t run iPad apps may even be a stance on the company’s part: An iPhone with an iPad Mini-like interior screen size isn’t that close a relative of the iPad Mini after all.

I do hope, however, that Apple is open to the possibility of the Duo’s influence rubbing off on future versions of iPadOS. The new iPad Mini’s hardware proves that the Mini is as relevant as ever. It’s still getting better at fulfilling the iPad’s original vision. The same should be true of the software it runs.

#10467. How Applicant Tracking Systems Read Resumes: 7 Checks Developers Should Make

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A resume that looks perfect on screen can look like a pile of scrambled text to a program. Applicant tracking systems (ATS) and hiring tools do not see your layout. They extract text from a file, guess where the sections are, and store what they find. If that step fails, the best experience in the world never reaches a human reader.

This matters whether you are sending your own resume or building a tool that processes thousands of them. This article walks through seven checks you can make with a few lines of Python, including one script you can run today.

A resume is a document with no guaranteed structure. One person writes "Work History", another writes "Experience", and a third uses a two-column layout that reads out of order. Any tool that processes resumes first has to turn this into predictable fields: contact details, sections, dates and skills.

Thinking in terms of a target schema helps. SitePoint's article on JSON schema design for document AI shows how the structure of the labels you define determines whether a document pipeline works. The same idea applies here: decide which fields you need before you write any extraction code.

Do not judge a resume by how it looks. Judge it by what comes out when you extract the text. SitePoint's walkthrough on building a JSON dataset from PDF documents describes a pipeline that extracts text from PDFs and then cleans up common extraction artifacts. That cleanup step exists because raw extraction is rarely tidy. Read the raw output first, and note what is broken.

PDF files store where characters are placed on a page, not what they mean. The pypdf documentation on extracting text from a PDF says this directly: there is no semantic layer, spacing is hard to get right because text is absolutely positioned, and pypdf is not OCR software, so it will never extract text from images.

Two practical results follow. A resume saved as a scanned image has no extractable text at all. And fancy layouts such as columns, text boxes and tables can come out in the wrong order.

Once you can extract text, a short script can catch the common failures. This one reads a PDF and reports whether it contains text, whether an email address is found, and which standard sections are missing:

Run it on a resume and look at the result. If has_text is false, the file is probably an image. If sections are missing, check whether your headings use unusual names. A passing result does not prove that every ATS will read the file the same way, but a failing result is a clear warning.

Different tools parse differently, so one script is only one opinion. A resume writing service such as ResumeCroc lists a free ATS resume checker among its tools, which is a quick way to see how another tool reads the same file. The linked page is a commercial service page, and every ATS behaves differently, so treat any checker as one data point and compare it with your own extraction results.

Most extraction problems come from layout. Single-column pages, standard headings, plain fonts and real text instead of images give parsers the best chance. Avoid putting contact details in headers or footers, since some tools skip them. If you are building a parser, test files with columns and tables on purpose so you know how your tool fails.

Create a folder of sample resumes: a clean one, a two-column one, a scanned one and one with unusual headings. Run your check against all of them whenever you change your parsing code. This turns "it seems to work" into something you can verify, and it makes regressions obvious.

Do all applicant tracking systems read PDFs the same way? No. Parsers differ in how they handle layout, headers and tables, so test more than one file format and more than one tool.

Is a Word file safer than a PDF? Not necessarily. Both can parse well or badly depending on layout. Test your own file instead of assuming.

Can a script guarantee my resume will pass an ATS? No. A script can find obvious problems, such as missing text or headings, but each hiring tool has its own rules.

Hiring tools read text, not layout. Define the fields you care about, extract the raw text first, understand what PDF extraction cannot do, automate basic checks, compare with an outside checker, keep formatting simple, and test against a small set of sample files. The script above is short, but it moves the question from "does it look right" to "what does a program actually see".

#10465. Engineer creates retro FPGA Game Boy console that can rip your cartridges to an SD card natively

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The project was nearly abandoned until a supportive retro enthusiast financed the equipment needed to bring it to life.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Joining the retro console revival movement is the SGBC, a new console that can play Nintendo Game Boy cartridges natively using an FPGA core. Built by Thomas Sanfourche, a French electronics engineer studying in Taiwan, the SGBC aims to give players a way to preserve and play their games with the full Game Boy experience, including a link port for multiplayer and trading.

The SGBC uses a Field-Programmable Gate Array (FPGA), a reprogrammable chip that can be configured to replicate the hardware logic of another chip, in this case, the Game Boy’s hardware. It can replicate not just the CPU, but multiple other components of a Game Boy as well, but Sanfourche has yet to specify which of the original Game Boy’s ICs have been consolidated into the FPGA.

The SGBC doesn’t just replicate the Game Boy as a console; it has plenty of features that retro gamers would appreciate — the most interesting among them being the built-in cartridge ripping function. If you insert your favorite Game Boy cartridge into the SGBC’s slot, you can preserve it by copying the ROM onto the microSD card in the console, digitizing your collection. It also outputs in 1080p 16:9 without stretching, and uses integer scaling to keep the game's pixels aligned with the pixels on your monitor or TV through HDMI. The SGBC also features an SNES controller port and a Game Boy Link port for multiplayer and trading.

Thomas Sanfourche almost ditched the SGBC entirely; in an interview with French retro gaming YouTuber GyuGyu, he said he went to Taiwan to study electronics engineering after discovering his strong interest in electronics. He didn’t know the local language and couldn’t enrol without certification, so he gave himself two years but ended up getting the language certification in six months. In his second year, he chose to enrol in the FPGA course after being intrigued by the name, and eventually chose it as the subject of his final-year project — a Game Boy FPGA core.

Sanfourche said his project was dismissed when he presented it to his professors: “What’s the point of doing this on an FPGA?” he was asked. This discouraged him and stopped him from working on it — until one day he went to a warehouse to buy a PVM monitor and struck up a conversation with a fellow customer who was a retro product seller and very active in the local retro scene. The discussion eventually led to his FPGA core Game Boy project, to which the seller asked, “Why don’t you market it?” The supportive stranger then offered, on the spot, to finance the equipment needed to market it or at least bring the project to life. The financier told Sanfourche he doesn’t want to be a part of the business, but that he wants to resell the product when he releases it, further supporting the project by sharing updates and teasers to local retro enthusiast Facebook groups.

The SGBC is yet to be released, but the project joins other FPGA consoles by the likes of retro gaming modding YouTuber Taki Udon’s SuperStation One, ModRetro’s M64, and Analogue’s SuperNT+ and MegaSG+. Not only are projects like these reviving the platforms, but they are also giving them modern features and comforts, bringing the question “What if these consoles were built today?” to reality.

#10462. Google shuts down a sneaky malware backdoor on millions of Android TV and Google TV boxes

Google has confirmed that an Android TV device maker snuck a backdoor for malware onto certified devices, which is leading to security improvements for the ecosystem as a whole.

In a post today, Google confirmed that it has cut ties with “Videostrong,” a company that was manufacturing certified Android TV boxes. This is due to the company building in a backdoor that it could sell access to, including the distribution of malware. Google has taken legal action to block the domains and servers that “Videostrong” was using to control the XCPE botnet and malware modules and banned the company and its subsidiaries from selling certified Android devices going forward, or selling software to any other certified vendors.

We found this malicious software built directly into many different device models and brands over multiple years, supported by dedicated infrastructure and constantly evolving configurations to evade detection and manage their affiliates. We believe this campaign wasn’t an isolated rogue insider or a development oversight. As a result, Google has cut ties with Videostrong and banned them from making devices with preloaded Google apps.

Synthient (via The Hacker News) compiled a list of potentially affected devices, with the focus being on the Mecool brand that has made some well-known Android TV and Google TV boxes in the past. Mecool has been rumored to be shutting down.

Google somewhat admits that this was a failing on its part, as these devices were sold as “certified” Android devices with the implication that they were safe. The company explains that these bad actors “employed obfuscation techniques to exploit a gap in our tests and load malicious code after the device’s first boot.” With that in mind, Google is updating its review process to protect users against this type of behavior, and Google reiterates that “a device manufacturer intentionally infecting their users with malware is an unacceptable breach of trust.”

Google’s shutdown of the domains and servers used to spread malware should leave affected Android TV and Google TV boxes safe to use – the company says that “taking down their C2 domains will sever access to at least 10 million additional compromised devices” – but owners may still want to consider alternate options.

Critical global infrastructure under threat

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

China is loitering over undersea cables in “gray-zone” warfare against neighboring countries, as well as the U.S. and its allies, as it seeks to dominate the South China Sea. Several incidents, reported by Nikkei Asia, suggest the PRC is actively mapping the seafloor for vulnerabilities and using civilian research vessels to do so.

A serious incident in September, for instance, saw the Philippine Coast Guard respond to a Chinese research vessel close to an undersea cable. The vessel reportedly ignored radio contact while broadcasting a false ID before manoeuvring to evade capture. The Philippines, in response, sent its largest vessel to respond. That vessel was spotted 37 nautical miles away from the Batanes province, officially part of the Philippines but claimed by China. The incident also saw a Chinese coastguard ship intervene, with Beijing complaining of harassment afterward, according to the South China Morning Post.

There have been no confirmed reports of Chinese attacks on undersea cables, but it isn’t the first report of Chinese attempts to map the region’s undersea infrastructure. A report by the Center for Strategic and International Studies, a U.S.-based think tank in Washington, DC, found that Chinese research ships were spending more time loitering near cables than any other nation. Their purpose for doing so is unclear, but the report made clear that the efforts were “disproportionate.”

One thing that the U.S. is certain about, however, is that China has the capability. A U.S. congressional report from 2025 highlighted that China is actively developing techniques to cut cables, pointing out that Chinese research teams had already publicly disclosed the details of electric cutting devices that could sever armored cables as deep as 13,000ft underwater.

Ryan Claffey, a research assistant for the Center for a New American Security, a think tank that specializes in U.S. national security, believes that a coordinated response is required to stop Chinese actions in the region. Writing in the report, Claffey warns that “subsea cables are digital lifelines,” arguing that “the U.S. and its allies must protect them in peacetime to stay connected in crisis.”

The People’s Republic of China has maritime disputes with several countries in the region. It continues to hold a claim over Taiwan, which it views as a breakaway province. It also has disputes with the Philippines, as mentioned earlier, along with Vietnam, Malaysia, and Brunei in the South China Sea, as well as Japan in the East China Sea.

Authoritarian nations like Russia, China and Iran have all stepped up operations against undersea infrastructure in Europe, Asia and the Middle East. Russia, for instance, has used fishing trawlers for nearly five years to loiter over communication cables in Europe. Iran, meanwhile, has threatened to attack undersea cables in the Strait of Hormuz if the U.S. attacks it again, describing them as legitimate targets during war.

#10458. How to Quickly Check What Your HTML Code Looks Like in a Browser

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Writing HTML is one of the main parts of building a website. It is mainly responsible for webpage structure and content.

A small error can result in layout problems. Oftentimes, you don't notice these issues until you preview the page in a browser. A quick way to ensure that your layout looks the same as you want is to preview your HTML code.

In this blog, we’ll show you easy ways to check how your HTML code will look in real time. So keep on reading till the end.

The following are two easy ways to check how your HTML code will look in real time.

This is a simple way to preview your HTML. You don't need a web server to preview your HTML code. You have to follow the following steps.

Open a code editor or simply Notepad on your device. Create a new file and paste your HTML inside it.

Step 2 - Save the File With an HTML Extension

After adding your HTML code in Notepad, save the file as index.html. You have to ensure that the extension is .html, not .txt.

If you are using Notepad on Windows, click on “File” and navigate to the “Save As” option and click it. Then enter index.html as the filename. Make sure that Notepad doesn’t save it as a text document.

Locate the saved file on your device. Double-click to open it in your default browser. The browser will render the HTML elements to show the headings, paragraphs, and buttons on the page, as you can see in the image below.

This is how you can create a simple file to preview your HTML before deploying it. If you see any errors, you can correct them in your file and preview it again.

However, it is worth considering that HTML may not show the complete website behavior alone. The final result can also depend on JavaScript, external CSS, or other resources. You should check those files as well.

This method provides an easy way to preview your HTML code. It involves using a specialized online HTML viewer. This approach is even quicker and doesn't even require you to create a local file first.

Step 1 - Open the HTML Viewer Open your web browser and search for an HTML viewer. You may see multiple tools in the search results. Select one that allows HTML preview online. For demonstration purposes, we are using HTML Viewer by minifier.org.

Once you’ve selected the tool, open it. Navigate to its input box and paste your HTML code into it. We are using the same HTML code from the first example.

You can also upload your file or paste its URL into the tool. After entering your HTML code, click on the “View” button to preview your HTML code.

In the screenshot above, you can see the tool showing the HTML preview on the right side. This tool also gives you the option to edit your HTML code and see the results in real time through the “Auto-update” option.

If the auto-update option is checked, you can see the changes in real time as you edit the code. This makes editing much easier, as you can modify the code and preview the changes immediately without returning to your original file. It also allows you to verify how your layout will appear on desktop, tablet, or mobile. You can check this by clicking on the icons provided in the top right corner of the tool. Once you are satisfied with the output, you can copy or download your edited HTML file easily.

This tool also allows you to test the HTML code of your game or video in real time, as you can see in the screenshot below.

These features make such specialized HTML viewers practical. These are quick and easy to use. However, the features shown above may not be available on all the tools. If you are specifically looking for such features, you can read the tool’s description for these features before using it.

HTML is one of the main components of a website. It defines the structure and content of webpages. Many users need to check how their HTML code will look in a browser to identify potential issues before deployment.

To preview your HTML code, you can use simple methods. One is to paste your code into Notepad and save it as index.html. Then open it in your browser to get a preview of your HTML code.

You can also use a specialized HTML viewer. Such tools can work by just pasting the code into the input box. Once you paste your code, click on the view button to get a preview of your HTML code. Some tools also offer various other features, such as editing, viewing games, and videos in real time.

Both of these methods work fine. The choice depends on the user's preferences and needs.

#10457. AAWireless expands beyond Android Auto adapters with new cooled Qi2 car charger

AAWireless is known for helping you convert a wired Android Auto connection into a wireless one, but the company is now launching a new product – an active-cooled Qi2 car charger.

“AAWireless Charge” is a Qi2 25W wireless car charger that costs $50. The charger is compatible with MagSafe, Pixelsnap, and Qi2 in general, and supports up to 25W charging speeds on compatible phones.

“Charge” also includes active cooling support with a built-in fan that the company says is “quiet” when active.

Included in the box you’ll find both an air vent mount as well as an adhesive base. There are also two magnetic ring adapters in the box for “Qi2 Ready” phones that don’t have magnets built-in or a magnetic case available. AAWireless says to use your car’s 12V outlet with the included adapter for “best performance,” but you can also use existing USB-C connections so long as they deliver enough power.

To be clear, there’s no wireless adapter built into the charger (as cool as that would be), but you can use this alongside an AAWireless adapter while driving, the company specifically mentions.

While AAWireless adapters remain available on Amazon, “AAWireless Charge” is available solely from the company’s website.

#10455. The Meta Quest 2 and Quest Pro are receiving their last OS updates soon. Here’s what you need to know

The best-selling VR headset of all time (and its Pro-level cousin) will no longer receive OS updates after March 2027.

Somehow, the Meta Quest 2 has been around for six whopping years, and Meta is finally bringing support for the headset to a close after March 2027. The far less popular Meta Quest Pro, which uses the same chipset as the Quest 2, will also see OS updates cease at the same time with OS v213. Meta will continue to deliver security updates through the end of 2027 to both headsets, according to an official Meta developers blog post.

Meta says it will not be removing cross-play multiplayer functionality or blocking access to Quest 2 players, so any games that support the headset will continue to work after these dates, so long as they don’t rely on any new OS features introduced after March 2027. Developers have to give players a 6-month notice if support is ending for a game, so you’ll have ample time to decide what to do.

That means the Quest 2 will receive just over 7 years of total security updates, while the Quest Pro gets two years less (since it came out in October 2022). The Meta Quest 2 remains the best-selling VR headset of all time, with around 20 million units sold over the lifetime of the headset. Meta says that Quest 2 owners can still play games throughout this time, although game support will likely dry up quickly after January 2028.

The $599 Meta Quest 3 and $349 Meta Quest 3S, which debuted in 2023 and 2024, respectively, are the follow-ups to the Quest 2. The upcoming Meta VR Glasses, which will retail for $1299, feature a next-generation chipset, a significantly higher-resolution AMOLED display, and substantially lighter weight and smaller size than either Quest 3 model.

Meta VR Glasses are expected to debut early next year, and with them, a new generation of OS development will take place. Since the Quest 2 uses a significantly outdated mobile chip at this point, it makes sense for Meta to cease new feature development for it and focus on newer, more capable hardware.

New power system is already operational in Ulanqab, China, where it will eventually ‘support up to one million AI accelerators.’

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Envision Energy says that it has completed the world’s first commercial data center deployment of an integrated 800-volt DC power system. It has been testing the Dubhe Controller, supported by its Dubhe Energy Foundation Model since August this year at its Galaxy AI data center in Ulanqab, Inner Mongolia, China. Touted as offering simpler, and more efficient power architecture, Envision’s system will also support higher computing density, says a press release. The Galaxy campus will scale to 3GW capacity according to current plans, and eventually “support up to one million AI accelerators.”

Currently, data centers rely on several power-conversion stages to achieve a usable supply from the grid. Envision’s Dubhe tech converts power directly from 10kV medium-voltage AC to an 800V DC bus to boost efficiency and slashes the size of the power conversion apparatus. It is built around three critical capabilities:

The system includes integrated battery support for fast response to AI workload spikes and for short-term backup power. It will also support higher-density AI racks, for more computing power in the same area, says the Chinese tech firm. Moreover, Envision’s system has been designed to work well with renewable energy sources and storage. Key to the efficiency and performance on offer is the AI-driven power management provided by the Envision Dubhe controller, smartly balancing power supply and computing demand. Other benefits include a more compact footprint and a reduced requirement for copper and other cabling.

By the firm’s own accounts the Dubhe tech system is performing well. Thus, it looks set to be instrumental to the expansion of the compute resources at the Galaxy AI data center in Ulanqab. Furthermore, Envision also intends to roll out this 800V DC tech at its Mission Gobi initiative, targetting 5GW of green AI data center capacity in desert regions by 2030.

#10450. AI Testing Tools: Benefits, Limitations, and Best Practices

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every vendor says the same thing: buy our AI, and testing gets easy. Don't believe it. AI testing tools do help, though. Some teams save real hours with them, while others waste a quarter and a budget. The difference usually comes down to how they start.

They support your QA team by writing test cases, running them, spotting failures and suggesting repairs. Some tools read a requirements document. Others watch real users click around and then replay what they did. Playwright, for instance, now ships built-in test agents: a planner that explores your app and writes a test plan, a generator that turns the plan into test files, and a healer that runs the suite and tries to repair failing tests.

Old-school automation needs a person to write every script. Then a developer moves one button and the whole thing falls over. Somebody loses a morning to repairs, and QA engineers hate that part of the job.

Quality is the big worry. Generated tests can look clever and prove nothing. A tool might confirm that a checkout page opens and then mark checkout as covered, but opening a page is not testing it. Even Playwright's documentation says generated tests may contain initial errors and that you should review what the healer changes before accepting it. A person has to read the output every time.

Business logic is the next problem. The tool has no idea that your refund window dropped from 30 days to 14, and it cannot feel that a signup form is confusing. Intent lives in people's heads.

Coverage numbers can mislead. A dashboard says 90%, and the room relaxes. Meanwhile, the odd edge cases nobody thought of sit untouched. Bugs do not read dashboards.

Check fit first. Does it cover web, mobile and API tests, and does it work with your stack? Ask before any sales call, not after. Then test its honesty: when something fails, a good tool tells you why. A vague red mark costs your team time because nobody can verify it. Read the pricing page slowly, too, since some vendors charge per seat and some per test run.

Finally, throw your ugliest feature at it. Demos use tidy little apps, but your product has pop-ups, slow pages and odd corners. Watch how the tool handles those.

Expecting instant results. People switch the tool on and wait for magic. What they get is noisy reports and grumpy engineers. Some managers even cut QA headcount right away, which is a bad idea: somebody has to tune the tool and handle the cases it cannot. Good testers become more valuable, not less.

Skipping training. Your team needs to learn how the tool behaves. Two short workshops and a shared doc go a long way.

Trusting machine-written text without a second look. AI tools now draft test descriptions, bug reports and release notes too. If your team wants to know how much of a document was machine-generated, an AI-content checker such as ZeroGPT can give a rough signal. Treat the result as a hint rather than proof, because such detectors make mistakes and publish their own accuracy claims.

Agents are the obvious next step. Instead of running fixed scripts, they poke around your app like a curious user, clicking, typing and reporting anything strange. Early versions already exist, and they are rough but improving.

Editors will change as well. Your code editor may soon suggest a test the moment you finish a function, and some bug reports may arrive with a fix attached. The wall between development and QA is getting thin.

Yes, but carefully. AI testing tools will not replace your QA team. They take the boring work off testers' hands and spot patterns people might miss, but they still need a human to say "that result is wrong." Start small, measure what happens and keep your testers close to the tool. Do that, and you will ship faster without losing control.

#10446. Android gaming is having a renaissance

Good morning! Today I’m mourning the loss of Patrick Stewart by rewatching First Contact, my favorite Trek movie; beginning a marathon listen of at least one great Star Trek podcast; and replaying Elite Force and Armada, both wonderful Trek games released in 2000.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

I’m starting this edition of Inbox a bit later than I’d like, but for a good reason: I got swept up by the subject I was researching. See, I wanted to write about the seeming renaissance of gaming on Android, which has always had clear advantages and disadvantages compared to iOS. On the plus side, its openness bred experimentation and a healthy indie gaming scene, along with a thriving emulation ecosystem born of its ability to sideload apps and gain access to Android’s underlying file system. On the downside, its device and OS version fragmentation has kept some developers from attempting challenging console and PC ports, and led others to abandon their titles to bugs and time.

But thanks to a confluence of factors, as well as form factors, Android gaming is in a very good place right now. Despite Google’s continued ambivalence towards paid and premium games, the platform has received official ports of console hits like Dredge and Sifu, with others like Replaced, Absolum, and Dispatch coming soon. (One could argue things have been good for a while with Stardew Valley, Dead Cells, Balatro, Slay the Spire, and Vampire Crawlers available already, but I digress.)

Most of these games are embracing a try-before-you-buy model to avoid the upfront cost that so many mobile users resist, while others, like Dispatch, are getting a mobile optimization that reorients the experience for a portrait display.

And while traditional emulation has existed since Android was young, recent months have seen considerable improvement in breadth and performance, aided by AI coding tools and a community that wants to play its library of Steam, Windows, and Switch games on phones. This rabbit hole is extensive and varied: WinNative combines work from Winlator Bionic and Pluvia to run Windows games on Android and connect PC storefront libraries. DroidDeck aims for a SteamOS-like experience, putting Steam’s Big Picture interface on supported Android hardware with Windows games running through ARM64 Proton, which debuted recently with the Steam Frame.

It’s via DroidDeck that I whiled away a couple of glorious hours on my Galaxy Z Fold 8 playing Lumines Arise, a game perfectly suited to the pick-up-and-play nature of smartphones (though it’s best with a controller). The latest Qualcomm chips have long outpaced the mobile games they’ve been asked to power, but few knew just how well they’d run demanding PC and console titles, even through compatibility layers like Proton.

Similarly, an Android branch of the increasingly popular nxnative, which compiles Switch and Switch 2 games for iOS and macOS, has been making it easy to play games like Super Mario Bros. Wonder and Metroid Dread at 60fps on many recent Qualcomm-powered devices. There’s even a Switch 2 version in the works for Android.

The big question is whether all of this works well: will people spend more time arguing with the software than playing the games themselves? And on the legal side of the business, will publishers find success selling discounted versions of AAA titles on Android now that the translation tools are better and the device compatibility much broader?

If there’s one thing I’ve learned covering Android for nearly 20 years, it’s that the community always finds a way.

ICYMI: Ben’s Weekender essay is all about how Motorola is filling the gap OnePlus left behind, mostly.

The strangest thing I’ve seen recently is the rumor that Elon Musk wants to launch a Starlink-branded terrestrial wireless carrier in the US. Now we know how he plans to do it: by purchasing Boost Mobile from EchoStar and leveraging its contracts with AT&T and T-Mobile along with new low- and mid-band spectrum Starlink recently acquired. That infrastructure, combined with Starlink’s existing array of LEO satellites, could make for a compelling advantage over the incumbents: namely, that dead spots practically won’t exist anymore.

But as we’ve learned from countless examples over the years, running a low-margin, high-churn postpaid wireless business is not for the faint of heart, and I’m not sure why Musk has decided that now is the right time to do it.

Five years on, is the Pixel 6 series really the disaster that we remember it being? The answer, according to Damien Wilde, is a bit more nuanced.

The Pixel 6 and Pixel 6 Pro were fundamentally flawed pioneers for Google. They were the firm’s most ambitious hardware undertaking to date, and simultaneously one of the most frustrating phone series to live with during their initial launch window and beyond. If the Pixel 5 was a safe play ahead of this big pitch, the Pixel 6 was a risky dive into deep waters. It stumbled badly. Yet without those painful growing pains, the more polished, confident, and reliable Pixel devices we enjoy today simply would not exist.

Finally, networked supermarket freezers in Sweden, Belgium, and Luxembourg were hacked over the weekend, sending temperatures up to a reported 50 degrees Celsius, and all the food inside them straight into the trash.

According to Reuters, “The latest incidents follow an attack in France last month when police said hackers seized control of cooling units at hundreds of supermarkets, causing fires and food losses.”

What we’re seeing with memory pricing right now is unprecedented. As we said earlier in the September quarter, we did see higher memory costs as compared to the June quarter, which were partly offset by the benefit of carry-in inventory and lower costs on certain non-memory components. For the December quarter, we expect even higher memory costs…Looking beyond the December quarter, we see the pricing for memory purchases continuing to increase.

John Ternus, Apple CEO, during the company’s Q4 2026 earnings call

Thanks for reading, and have a great day. I’ll talk to you on Thursday.

#10445. Google reveals new Gemini 4 Carbon AI model, narrowly beating Claude Opus 5.5

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Tests now push models to reveal their greater weaknesses.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Last week, Google released its latest AI model, Gemini 4 Carbon, topping intelligence rankings charts. The model has landed with a grander splash than most. As Artificial Analysis highlights, Gemini 4 Carbon is now the highest-ranked AI model on its latest Intelligence Index V5 benchmark suite, beating Claude Opus 5.5 by one point. It achieves the number one score across four of the nine evaluations, and does so at a lower cost per task than other high-ranking models, like Claude Opus 5.5. Google is offering access to the model for cyber defenders first through its Fairwind program, with wider availability coming sometime after.

Perhaps more crucially, Gemini 4 Carbon stands apart by topping Artificial Analysis' new AA-Omniscience v 1.1 test that tracks hallucinations and knowledgeable recall of information. As governments and institutions look to maximize value and security from their use of AI, being able to claim the lowest hallucination score could prove a significant selling point.

Then again, even the best scored only 54 on the -100 to +100 scale, suggesting even the best frontier models in 2026 are a far cry from being consistently reliable when it comes to accurately recalling information.

AI model rankings change fast and regularly in 2026, and it's no surprise that the latest release from a frontier lab has pushed the envelope, but Google's Gemini 4 Carbon can be seen as a comeback after languishing with irregular releases for much of the year.

Gemini 4 Carbon (high) scores a 59 on the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index V5 benchmark. That puts it a single point ahead of Claude Opus 5.5 (max with fallback), and two points ahead of OpenAI's GPT-6 Astra (max). It's also nine points ahead of Meta's Muse Wonder Full (Max), and 10 points ahead of Claude Fable 5.1 (max with fallback).

But raw, generalized intelligence isn't the whole picture with frontier models in 2026. As developers chase the Pareto frontier— the best-possible confluence of cost and intelligence — Gemini 4 Carbon is also impressively affordable. Its cost per task on the Intelligence Index test is actually slightly cheaper than Gemini 3.8 Flash (high), priced at $7.76 each. That's several dollars cheaper than either Claude Opus 5.5 or Sonnet 5.5, both max with fallback, and not far off half the price of Claude Fable 5.1, which costs $12.99 per task.

Gemini 4 Carbon is still more expensive than OpenAI's GPT 5 Astra and about six times more expensive than GPT 6.1 Sol (max). It's about 13 times as expensive per task as the open-weight DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017, too, although that model measures a mere 32 on the Intelligence Index rankings.

Gemini 4 Carbon isn't the best at everything, but it may be the best at most of the high price points. At least for now. Arena.ai has it holding the top spot on its Pareto Frontier chart at the more expensive end of the spectrum, but at lower price points, the Chinese open-weight models like GLM 5.3-Flash and DeepSeek V4 Pro take over.

Artificial Analysis also highlights that Gemini 4 Carbon isn't as good as Claude Code with Claude Opus 5.5 at programming, and it falls behind Grok 4.7, Muse Wonder, and Kimi K3.1 Pro on knowledge work.

But perhaps the most impressive score is Gemini 4's 54 on the AA-Omniscience Index, which tracks knowledge and hallucinations, beating out the competition by often significant margins.

Gemini 4 Carbon costs $7.76 per Intelligence Index task, ~23% less than Claude Opus 5.5 (max, $10.04) but ~1.2x GPT-6 Astra (max, $6.25). It uses 123k output tokens per task, 29% fewer than Claude Opus 5.5 (max, 173k). At the same $4/$20 standard pricing, its Cost per Task is in line with Gemini 4 Argon ($7.68), while scoring 9 points higher on the Intelligence Index.October 28, 2026

In Google's announcement of the new Gemini 4 model, it talked up its collaboration with the U.S. government for model screening and pre-release testing. In a similar manner to the way Anthropic handled Mythos' release in 2025, Google is rolling out access to Gemini 4 Carbon at a slower pace.

"We're taking a deliberate approach to rollout and are actively engaged in the U.S. government’s voluntary process for pre-release model testing," Google's launch statement reads.

To that end, it's opening up Carbon to a subset of "cyber defenders," and will gradually make the model more widely available through trusted partners through its Fairwind Program. Before it makes Carbon available to developers, it wants to iterate on its guardrails to make the AI safer and more effective for cybersecurity purposes, making it harder for nefarious actors to use it for exploits and hacking.

Where the idea of slowing the industry and introducing greater oversight for model development has risen and fallen in the estimations of the major tech companies, it does appear to be cementing itself as more of a foundational idea for future model releases. Google talks up its release on the improved safety and security of its model, highlighting that it thwarted all attempts from the Gray Swan Indirect Prompt Injection benchmark to have it do something it shouldn't.

Google claims Carbon cuts unsupported claims in its responses twice as much as the Gemini 4 Argon model it debuted just two weeks ago. If it can't complete an action, it will just not do so, rather than fabricating an answer or hedging around missing evidence, Google claims.

It also suggests Carbon will present sources and suggested actions to the user more readily, rather than pursuing its own intentions when there are discrepancies or uncertainties, allegedly cutting model overreach by 26%.

As governments and companies look to make realistic use of AI, tracking usage, managing token budgets, and preventing workers from creating new exploit paths through overuse, a model's safety, security, and reliability will be paramount.

Gemini 4 Carbon scores 54 on AA-Omniscience, the highest of any model, 6 points ahead of Claude Opus 5.5 (max, 48). It answers more questions correctly than Gemini 4 Argon (58% vs 48% accuracy), at the cost of a higher hallucination rate (11% vs 4%). This is still far below Claude Opus 5.5 (max) and GPT-6 Astra (max), both at 50%October 28, 2026

For the past year, a clear focus from frontier and open-weight model developers has been on efficiency and cost. While raising intelligence and improving capabilities, particularly through agentic workflows, has seen great development alongside pomp and circumstance, many of the major announcements and developments have also considered cost a clear component.

That's not likely to change, with the latest research from Gartner showing that just 3% of companies are actually achieving cost and productivity improvements from AI use. But as organizations push to find the real value in AI, maximize token budgets, and try to empower workers who have found improvements from using AI, they'll want consistency.

Tracking return on investment with AI is notoriously difficult, with hallucinations making that far harder. If Google can sell its latest model(s) as the safest and least likely to hallucinate, and do so with high intelligence at a relatively low cost, that might be the new winning formula.

#10443. Google’s Ocarina of Time easter egg is a blast from the past

If you’re sick of AI search and all the business of modern Google search, this week’s Google easter egg will take you back 28 years to a simpler time when Ocarina of Time was the pinnacle of gaming, and Google was still a new name.

It’s hard to believe I was sitting on a couch in my parents’ living room 28 years ago, playing Ocarina of Time on my Nintendo 64. Apparently, the folks at Google feel just as nostalgic as I do about the upcoming release of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time on the Nintendo Switch 2.

Case in point, if you go to Google right now and search for “Zelda” or “Ocarina of Time,” you’ll see a Master Sword appear in the bottom-right corner of your screen. Tap that sword, and you’ll be magically transported back to the way Google looked back in 1998. It’s just seven blue links with short descriptions on a very simple page. Even the “I’m Feeling Lucky” button works!

Unfortunately, the theme only works for this specific search, so you can’t keep things looking retro if you search for anything else, even if you start from the old-school search box on the 1998-themed page. Bummer. Tapping the Master Sword again brings you back to 2026 Google search, complete with a great little animation and sounds of Link in adult or child form, depending on which way you travel in time. Try it for yourself on desktop or mobile!
