#10442. Testing JSON-LD Like an API: 7 Checks Before Your Next Release

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A content editor changes an article’s headline. The page updates immediately, but its JSON-LD still contains the previous title. A redesign introduces a new author component while an old plugin continues publishing a different author name in the page source.

Neither failure necessarily produces a JavaScript error. Both leave machines and readers with conflicting descriptions of the same page.

For developers, the useful approach is to treat JSON-LD as a maintained output of the application, with a data source, validation rules, and regression tests. This guide uses a blog template to show how. You will need basic JavaScript knowledge and a modern Node.js installation for the runnable tests.

Requests for search improvements often arrive as broad outcomes: improve discoverability, clarify entities, or support answer engines. These need to become implementation requirements before development starts.

For example, AEO directory includes structured data within descriptions of broader optimization services. For an engineering team, the actionable deliverable is a page representation whose types, properties, and values can be checked against the published content. I personally used this , and adding this because this can help other developer's and designer for optimization and get higher search result's.

For a blog template, define these acceptance criteria:

Keep implementation success separate from search outcomes. Google’s AI features guidance says there is no special Schema.org markup required for its AI search features. Correct markup also does not guarantee rich results or inclusion in an AI-generated answer.

Avoid separate, manually maintained copies of the headline, author, and publication date. Both the visible template and metadata builder should consume the same published record.

Save the following as article-schema.mjs. The builder assumes an upstream content-validation step has already checked required strings, timestamps, and approved HTTPS URLs.

This is a deliberately small example, not a complete rich-result eligibility checklist. Add relevant images and other supported properties when the real page supplies them. Consult the Schema.org BlogPosting definition and Google’s Article documentation for their respective vocabularies and search guidance.

Use a consistent canonical-URL policy before building the identifier: normalize the intended hostname, trailing-slash convention, and query parameters. Do not generate a fresh UUID for the same article on every request.

SitePoint’s guide to implementing JSON-LD on modern websites covers embedding approaches across common stacks.

Valid JSON is not automatically safe to insert into a raw HTML script element.

A CMS value containing can terminate the element at the HTML-parser level, even when the value belongs to a JSON string. Concatenating a headline directly into a script tag is particularly risky.

Append this helper to article-schema.mjs:

JSON serialization handles quotes and backslashes. Replacing literal less-than characters prevents an embedded closing-script sequence from appearing in the resulting text. The Next.js JSON-LD guide documents this approach for script embedding.

Use the serialized result as the content of a script element with type="application/ld+json". Follow your framework’s documented embedding mechanism so the JSON is neither double-encoded nor inserted through an unrelated HTML context.

This helper is specific to JSON-LD script content. It is not a general-purpose HTML sanitizer, URL validator, or substitute for validating CMS data.

The tests should catch changes that would make metadata misleading or unsafe, not merely confirm that an object exists.

Save this file as article-schema.test.mjs beside the builder:

These are unit tests for the builder and serializer. They do not prove that a template embeds the script correctly or that the deployed page is eligible for a search feature. SitePoint’s explanation of unit, functional, and integration testing helps distinguish those responsibilities.

A passing builder test cannot detect a page component reading a different CMS field.

Add a browser-level check against a controlled article fixture. Inspect its visible heading, byline, dates, canonical link, and JSON-LD together. For this template, assert that the intended article entity appears once and matches those visible fields.

Do not assert that the entire page contains exactly one JSON-LD script. A site may legitimately publish organization, breadcrumb, and article entities separately. Instead, identify the relevant entity by its @id and detect conflicting definitions.

Include these failure cases in the test plan:

Prefer generating metadata from the content loaded during server rendering or static generation when that fits the application. If it is injected client-side, inspect the rendered result and test navigation explicitly. Avoid making essential metadata depend on a user interaction.

No single tool establishes syntax, semantic accuracy, search eligibility, and content consistency at once.

Test both representative code samples and deployed URLs. A validator cannot confirm the truth of an author name simply because the property is syntactically valid.

Google’s general structured-data guidelines also require markup to represent the page accurately. Treat validation warnings as findings to investigate; do not add invented reviews, dates, or attributes merely to improve a tool’s output.

Ownership matters after the first release. Decide which application layer generates article markup, who maintains its mapping, and which publishing events invalidate cached output.

When an editor changes a headline, the visible page and JSON-LD should be rebuilt or refreshed together. Updating one while serving the other from an older cache creates a mismatch even if both implementations are individually correct.

Avoid setting dateModified to the current time on every deployment. A CSS update or infrastructure rebuild does not necessarily mean the article changed. Read the timestamp from the editorial record instead.

Before shipping a template change, run the builder tests, inspect one rendered page per affected template, and check representative deployed URLs. Keep a small fixture set that includes optional fields, long text, translated content, and script-like strings.

A reliable JSON-LD implementation has a traceable relationship with published content. When someone changes the page, its machine-readable description should change predictably—and the test suite should expose the cases where it does not.

#10440. Xbox’s former COO has ‘serious concerns’ about her abrupt departure

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Helen Chiang is setting the record straight on her sudden exit from Microsoft.

Helen Chiang is setting the record straight on her sudden exit from Microsoft.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Xbox’s former chief operating officer, Helen Chiang, suddenly left Microsoft less than two weeks ago after being promoted into the role in July. I wrote in my Notepad newsletter last week that it seemed very weird for Chiang to suddenly depart Microsoft after 21 years with a surprisingly short memo from Xbox CEO Asha Sharma. Now, Chiang says she has “serious concerns” about how she was treated.

In a lengthy LinkedIn post — entitled “Clarity is Kindness,” a reference to Sharma’s WSJ profile — Chiang doesn’t dispute that she was fired, but she doesn’t say why she was let go. Eurogamer reported last month that Chiang had been “terminated for policy violations,” but she says this is “not accurate.”

Chiang denies unfounded rumors that speculated she was leaking confidential information, or that she left to join a competitor. It’s clear the decision to leave Xbox was out of her hands. “You don’t spend two decades somewhere without developing a deep loyalty to the people, teams, and businesses you’ve helped build,” says Chiang. “Xbox was never just another stop in my career. My end game was always to keep building here.”

She also denies engaging in “discrimination, bullying, harassment, insider trading, embezzlement, or other fraudulent activity.” Like I wrote in Notepad last week, Chiang has been described to me by multiple Xbox employees as highly respected, with high integrity. “I stand by my record: both the results I delivered and the way I have treated people throughout my career,” says Chiang.

The most significant part of Chiang’s post is a paragraph on fairness and accountability, hinting that she wasn’t treated fairly:

I believe in fairness, accountability, and holding ourselves to the standards we expect of others. Those standards only mean something when they’re applied consistently. I have serious concerns about how they were applied in my case, and I believe those concerns deserve to be taken seriously.

Chiang also says “This is not how I ever imagined my story here would end. And it is not the whole story.”

We’ve reached out to Chiang to ask if she’s pursuing legal action against Microsoft. We’ve also asked Microsoft to comment on Chiang’s post, but the company didn’t respond in time for publication.

#10438. Redmagic 12 Pro review: You’ve never gamed on a phone like this before

The Redmagic 12 Pro impresses with a Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6 processor, liquid cooling, a fan, plus a massive 8,400mAh battery to keep the games going for hours and hours.

As I sat in my living room with my family, playing Monster Hunter Outlanders together on the Redmagic 12 Pro, I completely forgot I was playing on a phone. The game looked perfect. It ran at a locked 60FPS and hadn’t dropped frames since I began playing over an hour ago. It just felt like I was back in college, playing Monster Hunter Freedom on my PSP with my friends in the same room.

Most other phones at this point would have throttled hard. What began at 60FPS almost certainly would have been 40FPS tops after 30 minutes or so; the frame drops during heavy fights would have been even worse, but the Redmagic 12 Pro didn’t skip a beat. Even after a solid hour of playing, the battery had only dropped 10%, completely erasing all concerns about using my phone as a gaming console.

This thing is the real deal, and while I’ve used plenty of high-performance phones and gaming phones over the years, none have quite nailed it as this one has. Now, with my Redmagic 12 Pro and my trusty GameSir G8 Galileo, I can game whenever and wherever I want without battery anxiety or thermal problems. Redmagic has gotten really good at this gaming thing.

Sustained gaming performance is difficult to pull off on a phone, which is why the Redmagic 12 Pro features two types of active cooling: a fan and liquid cooling. This design builds on last year’s with a larger liquid cooling system and a more efficient fan. The intake and exhaust vents are on the sides of the phone, not the back, ensuring these shouldn’t get easily blocked. Despite this, the phone is IP68 and IP69 rated.

Most modern phones have huge camera islands that make them awkward to use in controllers, but the Redmagic 12 Pro features a completely flat build that fits perfectly in *any* controller. I don’t usually like overly flat builds, but it actually suits this phone’s purpose.

This should mean the phone does exceedingly well in benchmarks, but I have the distinct feeling the Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 6 processor inside is making that overly difficult. I don’t have any other SD8 Elite Gen 6 phones just yet, but rumor has it that this is one *hot* chipset. I saw the phone hit 50C during testing, so that definitely seems to be the case.

In 3DMark Solar Bay Extreme, a benchmark that stresses ray-tracing performance over 20 minutes, the Redmagic 12 Pro started at 2065 points and ended at 1169 on its last run. That’s a 43% drop in performance over a 20-minute period, which is in line with passively-cooled phones like the Galaxy S26 Ultra. Regardless, the initial performance is nearly double the S26 Ultra, while the 1169 end score matches the S26 Ultra’s best score and is roughly 35% higher than the S26 Ultra’s sustained performance score of around 860.

Actual gaming seemed to fare better in my testing, where I saw sustained performance consistently outperform any Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5 phone I have in my possession. When running Wanderstop through the phone’s built-in Steam emulation (more on that soon), the Redmagic 12 Pro averaged 72FPS at the highest settings. Other phones with SD8 Elite Gen 5 processors scored half that, at best. Native Android games like Monster Hunter Outlanders worked even better.

But it’s not just performance that sets this phone apart; it’s the gaming features. Redmagic has fully leaned into the mobile gaming power user angle with its software. Pressing and holding the red button on the right side of the phone launches Gravity X, the built-in game launcher that keeps all your games in one place. It even features an embedded Steam emulator, so all you need to do is sign in with your Steam account and download anything in your library.

Overall performance on Steam games is impressive, but like any emulation, not everything is going to work. Hogwarts Legacy worked near-flawlessly at medium settings, but titles like Star Wars Galactic Racer wouldn’t run at all. Other apps that run Steam games, like DroidDeck and Game Native, also work, but the Adreno 850 GPU doesn’t have official support on those apps yet, so compatibility is very hit or miss (mostly miss) for now.

The Redmagic 12 Pro’s in-game menu is also unlike anything you’ve ever seen. It’s not just jam-packed with information, but has a boatload of bespoke features. One custom mapping tool lets you map the triggers (yes, this phone has capacitive triggers on the side) to any on-screen virtual key.

Another utility records all the time, Xbox/PS5/Switch style, and saves a clip at the touch of a floating button. Yet another features on on-screen AI anime character that can interact with your games and even give you gameplay feedback. You can even enable frame gen and Super Resolution in any game. It’s wild.

All this is wrapped up in an incredibly impressive hardware package, with loud, clear speakers, an excellent 185Hz AMOLED display (that uses DC dimming and 2160Hz PWM for eye comfort), with 5,000-nit peak brightness. I measured the actual HDR brightness at 2300-nits, so this can get nice and bright when it needs to.

If there was ever a phone that could play games and still get you through a full day’s normal phone usage, it’s this one. Passthrough charging is also supported, too, so you can keep the battery from getting overcharged or overheated while gaming for hours.

As is the case with many laser-focused devices like this, the camera is the low point of the experience. It’s got a 50MP rear camera and a 50MP ultra wide. Both get the job done, but they’ll never impress you.

The 16MP 9th-generation under display camera looks impressive on the surface — you can’t see it at all, even with light shining off the display — but the results are still far from good. Redmagic uses an AI algorithm to substantially enhance the overall quality, which cleans up the image nicely but exhibits lots of hallucinations. Just look at my eyes in the above picture. It’s not good.

Thankfully, the Android 17-based Redmagic OS 12 software is quite good. Visually, it reminds me of a mix of AOSP and Samsung’s One UI. Lots of expected features are here — an AI wallpaper generator, call screening, recording with transcripts, writing help, etc — plus all the awesome gaming-focused tools and quite a bit of customization across the OS.

It’s a great overall experience, but Redmagic only offers software updates for 3 years, so this one won’t stay around as long as some other phones. If you’re more interested in mobile gaming than anything — especially if you want a phone that can easily run Steam games in addition to mobile stuff — this is an impressively good choice.

#10435. Google has fixed Android Auto issues on Samsung Galaxy phones running One UI 9 [U]

Samsung’s Android 17-based One UI 9 update is rolling out more widely and, with it, some apparent issues with Android Auto on some Galaxy phones.

Update 11/3: Speaking to 9to5Google, Google has confirmed that “a fix has been rolled out” for Android Auto issues on Galaxy devices running One UI 9.

In the time since Samsung’s stable Android 17 update started rolling out, there have been reports popping up around Android Auto issues. Reports of these problems have shown up on Reddit, Samsung’s forums, and Google’s forums.

It’s not a massively widespread problem as far as we can tell, but there’s a clear pattern and enough reports to suggest a trend. Those affected report issues with stuttering performance and/or the connection failing entirely.

The folks at Android Authority found one user test where a Galaxy S25 Edge on One UI 8.5 lacked any issues, but a Galaxy S24 Ultra running One UI 9 immediately reproduced the issues that other users have been reporting. It’s hardly a scientific test, but it shows that One UI 9 is probably responsible for some of this odd behavior.

There’s been no official word on a fix just yet. Let us know in the comments below if you’re dealing with any issues.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Anthropic has updated Claude Code’s system prompt to prevent the AI tool from being used to re-create existing video games, as reported by Tom’s Hardware. Retro gaming is currently being overrun with AI-made decompilations, recompilations, and ports, but while Anthropic will still let people use Claude Code to make games, it doesn’t want people to use it for direct clones.

Last week, a user on X posted a screenshot allegedly showing two new sentences added to Claude Code’s system prompt: “Do build original games, games in a genre, and games from the user’s own designs and assets. Don’t build a recreation of a specific existing game, even if it is named or called original.”

Anthropic’s Addy Osmani, in a reply, didn’t confirm that specific language but did say that the company had updated the prompt in Claude Code on Opus 5.5:

We added two sentences to Claude Code’s prompt on Opus 5.5 to steer it away from recreating someone else’s specific game because using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy.

Original games, genre games, and games built from your own designs and assets are meant to keep working.

We’ll continue to iterate to ensure we’re striking the right balance. In the meantime, if Claude turns down something that’s yours, send it my way.

We’ve asked Anthropic if it has or will be updating other Claude system prompts as well.

#10432. How to Create an App in ChatGPT in 2026

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Learning how to create an app in ChatGPT starts with the Jotform App Builder plugin (listed in ChatGPT's plugin directory as Jotform Apps): install it, connect a Jotform account, describe the app, approve the requested action, and refine the result. The plugin creates a Jotform App containing pages, forms, and resources that can be edited in Jotform App Builder and shared with its intended audience.

ChatGPT helps turn an idea into a clear request and supports revisions through conversation. The Jotform App Builder plugin performs the app-building actions, while the App Builder in Jotform provides the editor for closer customization.

This no-code ChatGPT app builder workflow suits projects built around information, forms, and everyday tasks.

"ChatGPT app development" can also describe coding projects. OpenAI's documentation on building plugins for ChatGPT describes a package of skills, an optional MCP server that connects to tools and data through the Model Context Protocol, optional UI, authentication, and publishing. The two routes differ like this:

This article covers the no-code route. The result is a Jotform App; the workflow does not include writing and deploying a separate codebase.

A Jotform App can organize pages, forms, and resources for customers, employees, members, or event attendees. The most useful first project has one audience and one clear purpose. Here are some examples:

These are starting structures. A booking form, for example, still needs its availability settings checked. An ordering app needs its order process reviewed, and any payment setup requires separate attention.

Setup starts with installing the plugin in ChatGPT and authorizing access to the intended Jotform account. Jotform's own how-to guide lists the steps.

To create an app in ChatGPT, select the installed plugin in a new chat and enter a specific description. Include the audience, purpose, pages, and design direction.

This gives the AI app builder a defined structure. The studio should then supply its real booking URL, approved resource links, contact details, and onboarding copy during review. It is important to review every generated form, link, and business rule before use.

A good prompt explains who will use the app, what they need to accomplish, and which elements support that task. Visual preferences help, but purpose and content should come first.

"Build an app for my business" leaves the audience, structure, and actions unclear. The design-studio example identifies a client audience, an onboarding task, specific pages, and a color scheme. Before submitting a request, define:

Request the essentials to build a Jotform App around one process, then expand.

The plugin supports follow-up requests in the same chat. Smaller changes make it easier to review each update and identify anything that needs correction. For the studio onboarding app, some follow-ups could include:

Each request should be followed by a review of the result. A map needs the correct address; a gallery needs suitable images; a booking form needs appropriate fields and configuration.

Select Edit in Jotform when you need closer editing. In Jotform App Builder, inspect every page, element, form, link, label, and visual choice. Replace the sample content and check that navigation follows the client's expected path. Third-party integrations require manual setup in the builder, so requesting a booking feature should not be treated as confirmation that an external calendar has been connected.

Select Share App in the generated result to open the available sharing options. For detailed distribution and access settings, open the app in Jotform App Builder and use the Publish tab and Quick Share. Jotform's sharing guide covers each option.

The builder supports copying the app link, inviting people by email, social sharing, and downloading a QR code. Choose the method that fits your audience: a client can receive a link, while event attendees may find a QR code more useful.

Review access before distributing the app, because newly created apps are public by default. Check exactly what a visitor can open before publishing.

Test the Jotform App as an intended user would, using fictional information first. Review it on both desktop and mobile before collecting real submissions.

Creating an app with the plugin is free to start, and Jotform offers a free Starter plan, but app usage remains subject to your account's allowances. A Jotform support reply from 2024 describes Starter as up to five forms and 100 monthly submissions. These are account allowances, rather than a separate allocation for each generated app, and they may have changed, so confirm them on Jotform's current pricing page. Paid plans provide more submissions.

Before you launch, compare expected usage with the current account limits. An onboarding app serving a few clients has different requirements from an event app collecting hundreds of registrations.

The most common mistakes are unclear requests, oversized first versions, and sharing before testing. Each can be reduced by defining a smaller task and reviewing the result.

This workflow is best for defined business tasks. Projects requiring extensive custom backend behavior need a separate assessment, such as the developer route described above.

Before sharing, confirm that the app serves its intended audience and that its content, behavior, and settings have been reviewed. A short launch checklist helps keep the review consistent.

Here are some frequently asked questions about the output, account requirements, and practical limits of the Jotform App Builder plugin.

Yes. With the plugin installed and authorized, plain-language requests can generate and revise an app in ChatGPT.

The plugin can create a Jotform App combining pages, forms, and resources. Follow-up prompts can refine its structure, content, and design.

Customer portals, internal tools, event apps, business websites, directories, onboarding hubs, and booking or ordering apps are some of the suitable starting points.

A Jotform App is the finished app created and maintained through Jotform. A custom GPT configures a conversational assistant, while a plugin or application made with OpenAI developer tools involves a separate development workflow with its own build, authentication, and publishing steps.

Understanding how to create an app in ChatGPT means keeping the roles clear: ChatGPT supports the conversation, the plugin creates and revises the Jotform App, and the Jotform editor handles closer editing and configuration.

Start with a focused task, then review the content, permissions, and behavior. Test the complete experience with sample information before sharing it with the intended audience.

Cloud providers have been told that they must sell 51% of their services to non-Chinese companies for the first time.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nvidia has purportedly told its data center customers that they must limit the amount of business that they do with Chinese companies in what is a new policy for the U.S. company. The move would mean that data center operators who sell their services to Chinese companies must now track what proportion of their overall business they make up, limiting them to just 49% of the total and ensuring 51% are non-Chinese outfits. The FT reports the new policy has been communicated to Nvidia's customers by its sales force and regional teams, and does not appear to have been laid out in any official form via documentation.

Notably, Nvidia's alleged new stance on Chinese customers does not only apply to data centers that are located in Asia. Instead, it applies to those based globally, and even then it only affects new and future contracts rather than any existing Nvidia hardware leasing agreements that are in place. In a statement, Nvidia notes that "American chips are the foundation that American model and application makers need to compete worldwide." The statement goes on to say that current U.S. policy "encourages" cloud providers to build on American infrastructure and to accept business from "everywhere they can."

But this latest move to restrict Chinese access to Nvidia's high-performance data center hardware comes amid ongoing tensions between the United States and China with regard to AI capabilities. Nvidia can only sell its chips to whitelisted companies, a situation that has led to a thriving black market for its wares. Earlier this year, it was found that even Nvidia's older servers have become so sought-after that their values have tripled.

Whether limitations on who Nvidia can sell its chips to have had the desired effect is open to debate, with even China's state-owned telecoms giant China Telecom having recently been found to have Nvidia hardware in its data centers. The situation wouldn't be out of place in a blockbuster espionage thriller, with Nvidia CEO Jason Huang reportedly the target of a spy plot by Chinese agents.

The reasons behind the U.S. government's attempts to limit China's access to Nvidia hardware are obvious. The aim is to stymie China's ability to advance its own AI technologies, with Nvidia chips the go-to option for AI servers and data centers around the globe. But this limitation on which customers cloud service providers can accept will put additional strain on companies that are already fighting it out for the same contracts. Reducing the pool of potential customers could potentially see a shrinking in the scale of data center operations moving forward.

#10428. AI, DevOps and the Cost of Hosting: From Idea to Production for $0

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI coding agent rebuilt my photo-collage app as a distributed system and deployed it to a free server. The hard part turned out to be hardware: one free ARM CPU, 538 collages created during load testing, zero failed jobs, and $0 a month.

Like most developers, I have never been short of product ideas. Hosting them has always been the problem. I have tried shared hosting with SmartWeb, Azure free credits, Google Compute Engine’s free VM instances, SmartWeb’s virtual private servers, and Heroku dynos. None of them left me satisfied. The worst experience was a $200 charge for an AI address API on Google Cloud—for an application that never earned me a single kobo.

I am not a cheapskate, but I learned quickly that one of the easiest ways to kill a potentially good idea is to get the finances wrong. An application has to stay online until it reaches a critical mass of users, and hosting an untested, unverified product on expensive hardware can be wasteful.

Agentic AI coding has changed how quickly I can move from idea to production. Over about five weeks of intermittent coding—amounting to roughly three days of full work with Claude—my photo-stitching app went from an Angular 8 project that would not even build to a distributed system running in production.

Along the way, Claude upgraded the front end from Angular 8 to Angular 22 and fixed the build on Node 24. It set up RabbitMQ with Docker Compose so the message broker configures itself on startup, and added real-time notifications over SignalR with a progress spinner. It fixed a crash loop caused by an unreadable iPhone photo. It redesigned the system so any server can process any job, with switchable database and file storage, direct browser-to-storage uploads, and photos resized in the browser before upload. It added reliability work that a team might debate for a sprint: a dead-letter queue, confirmed publishing, idempotent workers, recovery of stuck jobs, and automatic clean-up. It fixed the stitching so photos keep their proportions and borders are even. It wrote 41 automated tests and scrubbed leaked passwords and personal photos from the Git history.

Coding, QA, DevOps, and production support used to be separate phases, often handled by separate people. Here they happened in one conversation. When code becomes this cheap to produce, the bottleneck moves. For me, it moved to hardware.

To check what the AI builds, you have to run it locally. A distributed app means running the whole system locally: a web app, a background worker, a message broker, a database, and storage emulators. Because Claude adds features quickly, and each one can add another container, it is easy to exhaust a laptop’s RAM and CPU.

My setup made this lesson concrete. I work on a MacBook Air, with Visual Studio in a Windows virtual machine. Docker cannot run inside that VM on Apple Silicon, so Claude ran Docker on the Mac and drove it from Windows over SSH. Later, my mobile connection started dropping large packets and broke uploads from the VM, so Claude moved the load testing onto the server itself. The software problems took minutes. The hardware and network problems took longer.

Then comes hosting. Heroku looked like the easy route, but it has had no free dynos since 2022. The cheapest option is an Eco dyno at about $5 a month, plus about $5 for Postgres. That is affordable, but running it for an idea that has no users yet did not seem prudent.

Claude suggested Oracle Cloud’s Always Free tier: an ARM server with up to 4 CPUs and 24 GB of RAM, free with no end date. I created a small instance with 1 CPU and about 6 GB. Claude wrote a production Docker Compose file and a one-command setup script: Caddy as the gateway, the web app, the stitching worker, RabbitMQ, and PostgreSQL, with data that survives restarts. My repository is private, so rather than put a GitHub token on a public server, it copied the code over SSH and deployed it. The last blocker was mine to fix: opening ports 80 and 443 in Oracle’s network firewall. Then the site was live.

I did not want to guess, so I asked Claude to load-test the live server. Simulated users ran the full flow: upload two photos, wait for the real-time notification, and download the stitched collage.

The server handled 64 people creating collages at the same moment without a single failed job: 538 collages, zero errors. Its ceiling was about 62 collages a minute—one per second. Beyond that, users queue. With 4 people creating at once, 95% of collages arrived within about five seconds; at 64, each person waited about a minute. If each active user makes a collage every couple of minutes, one CPU comfortably serves around 85 people at once, and a few hundred if they use it less often.

The test also showed what runs out first, and it is not the CPU. Each collage stores about 5 MB, mostly the PNG output, so at full speed the disk would fill in under two hours. Switching to JPEG and shortening retention fixes that, and when traffic grows, three more free CPUs are waiting. That is the value of measuring before paying: I know what to change, and when.

Oracle isn’t being charitable. Servers need applications to run—ideally ones that go viral—and the free tier is a play for loyalty. Oracle wants successful projects to stay and grow into its paid services instead of moving off the platform once they reach critical mass. It can also reclaim free instances that sit idle.

I think the bet is smart, and it points to a bigger trend. AI-generated code still needs energy to run and hardware to host it, even when far fewer people work on each project. As more people turn ideas into working software, demand for compute will keep growing, and I expect hosting and data centres to become a very profitable business.

For now, the combination works: Claude orchestrates the coding, QA, and DevOps, and Oracle’s free server keeps an idea online long enough to find out whether it deserves to grow.

#10423. Galaxy Z Fold 8 sales spike amid iPhone Duo release and ‘crackgate’ debacle

The iPhone Duo’s tumultuous debut seems to have some buyers looking to Samsung’s Galaxy Z Fold 8 instead, as sales spiked in the week following Apple’s release.

The release of iPhone Duo was October 23 and, according to The Korea Herald, that same week is when sales of Samsung’s Galaxy Z Fold 8 spiked.

The specifics here are that sales of Samsung’s Galaxy Z Fold 8 went up by 20% in Korea from October 23 through October 29. Exact numbers weren’t shared, but it does show a trend in Samsung’s foldable getting more attention in the wake of Apple’s first attempt at the form factor. In the days following Apple’s September announcement of the Duo, interest in Galaxy Z Fold 8 went up as well.

There are any number of reasons why customers might be turning their attention to Samsung’s device. It could be the continued sales that make the Fold 8 a better deal overall, especially in Korea where the Fold 8 is considerably more affordable compared to the Duo. Other factors at play could include the messy implementation of unoptimized apps in Apple’s software, or even the iPhone Duo’s “crackgate” debacle that left a rough first impression, as a number of buyers were left with broken inner displays due to an apparent manufacturing error. There’s no evidence either way here yet, but there are plenty of potential reasons.

Galaxy Z Fold 8 is currently available for as low as $1,599, with Samsung rumored to be investing more heavily in the new form factor in 2027.

#10422. Bambu Lab H3C 3D printer review: A nine-tool monster

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

The Bambu Lab H3C is an excellent nine-tool 3D printer that printed everything we could throw at it. Whether you want nine-color PLA models, mix and match engineering filaments, or crazy squishable TPU shoes in a riot of color, this machine is a beast. If you don’t have low ceilings or a fear of step ladders, the H3C might be the only printer you’ll ever need.

Works with multiple materials and nozzle sizes in the same print

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

The Bambu Lab H3C is the printer we’ve been waiting for: a true Bambu tool changer. It has nine “Swaptek” tools that use a technology similar to the H2C Vortek rack, except each nozzle is built into a lightweight, hardwired tool with a dedicated filament path. The H3C is Bambu’s fastest multicolor 3D printer, and though it reduces waste, it does not eliminate it.

The H3C comes with a surprising “old school” technology: the ability to print 2.85mm filament. This is in response to China’s increasing fascination with 3D-printed shoes made with a wide variety of squishy TPU. Bambu sent along a few spools of their new super soft 75A TPU to show off, and it does make for comfy footwear. Each unit comes with one special 2.85mm tool as a standard feature, though you can buy a “normal” 1.75mm tool for $59 if you’re not into the novelty of phat filament.

You will also be able to add cutting, drawing, embossing, and foil transfer tools to the system. These were not available at the time of review, but we’ve seen these accessories on the H2D and A2L. The 3D printer will NOT come with a laser variant.

Bambu is checking off yet another item from the user wish list: the ability to print with more than one nozzle size. Because this is more of a software upgrade, you’ll also gain the ability to mix nozzles with Bambu’s other dual nozzle systems: the H2D, H2C, and X2D. The H2D will get the ability first, and the other printers will be updated by March.

The H3C has a 330 x 320 x 325mm build volume (the same as the H2C) and a massive “AMS” type module perched atop the main unit that holds nine spools. This gives it a somewhat towering mushroom shape that’s over three feet tall. It did not fit on my studio counter, which is a standard-sized IKEA kitchen countertop with a filament rack three feet off the surface. When we parked it on my industrial-grade shelving unit next to the H2D, even my 6’4” husband needed a step stool to load filament. Bambu was prepared for this problem and created a special rolling cart for the machine to make it more accessible. This is the first time the company has offered custom furniture for one of their printers and is an optional purchase that I highly recommend.

All nine tools are integrated into the bottom of the AMS 3H, which means the H3C only comes as a nine-tool 3D printer. There is no such thing as a single-color H3C. If nine colors aren’t enough for you, the printer can be hooked up to as many as four “normal” AMS units with the help of an optional buffer. Unfortunately, all outside AMS units need to run through the 5th tool slot, so you will lose the purge-free perk of the H3C.

The AMS 3H does not have motorized rollers, as retraction is unneeded on a tool changer. Seven of the slots have a TPU bypass, something we first saw on the single spool AMS HT. Two of the slots are nestled within an interior chamber for drying at up to 85°C. Though you can only dry two spools in the AMS 3H, you can run the dryer while printing, as it has its own power supply. The sealed dryer does not “leak” enough heat to dry the rest of the chamber…we checked. But that also means you don’t have to worry about the dryer melting a spool of PLA left in a nearby slot.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The FlexFeed extruder mounted on the toolhead is amazing. It can self-adjust to handle normal 1.75mm PLA or expand to push 2.85mm TPU quickly and without issue. The Bambu Lab H3C comes standard with eight .4mm nozzles for 1.75mm filament and one .4mm nozzle for 2.85mm filament. The nozzles are held in place with three screws and are pretty easy to swap out. You do need an entirely separate tool (with larger openings) for the 2.85mm filament. Because the H3C can print with multiple nozzle sizes, you could leave one or more tools dedicated to .2, .6, or .8 nozzle sizes if that fits your workflow.

For example, slots 6 and 8 are located inside the dryer and have a TPU bypass. You could dedicate a .6mm nozzle to these tools to better handle TPU, something like PA CF, which both benefit from toasty dry filament and an extra-wide nozzle. All nozzles for the H3C are hardened steel (HRC 55+) by default.

My H3C did have some pre-launch issues and is running Beta firmware that was updated several times during the review. The printer developed an annoying clicking sound coming from the left-side stepper motor, and one of the tools has a tendency to tangle and catch on the other Bowden tubes, causing it to dismount unexpectedly – and annoyingly – during long multicolor prints. I printed a few TPU spacers to try and solve this problem without much success as of yet.

The Bambu Lab H3C is launching with a retail price starting at $2,499 for all nine tools (eight standard 1.75mm and one 2.85mm). This is only $100 more than the launch price of the H2C. Additional tools and nozzle sizes will be available, though we do not have all the pricing at this time. A bundle that includes the Bambu Station (rolling cart) starts at $2,799. This makes the machine a bit on the spendy side, but for a helmet-class, honest-to-goodness tool changer with Bambu Lab’s ease of use, this 3D printer has earned its place on our Best 3D Printer of 2026 list.

330 x 320 x 325 mm (12.99 x 12.59 x 12.79 inches)

PLA/TPU/ABS/ASA/PA/PC/PET/PPS/Fiber reinforced (up to 350 degrees)

PEI textured spring steel sheet (120 degrees)

493 x 571 x 1016 mm (19.40 x 22.48 x 40 inches)

The Bambu Lab H3C comes with a toolkit and the cables needed to assemble your 3D printer, as well as a few spare parts. The printer is preloaded with files, including a scraper that you can build yourself with the included parts. There’s also a quick start guide for both the base unit and the AMS 3H.

For the review, Bambu Lab provided spare printer tools for both 1.75mm and 2.85mm filaments, as well as several spools of PLA Palette and 2.85mm TPU 75A. We also received a Bambu Station for the review.

The H3C is an incredible piece of hardware. The Swaptek system has nine tools, each with its own filament path, heater, and hardened steel nozzle. The FlexFeed extruder has cooling and everything else. Each nozzle stays loaded and is preheated for lightning-fast filament swaps. I clocked it at six seconds from printing one color to printing the next.

The tools and nozzles come in both 1.75 mm and 2.85 mm filament sizes. Each tool has a nozzle attached by three screws, which can be swapped with different sizes. The H3C ships with one 2.85mm tool, but you have the option to purchase more tools if you want to customize your configuration. Though not ready at the time of this review, Bambu Lab will be offering tools for cutting, drawing, debossing, and foil transfer at a later date.

The tools dock on a front-mounted rack that retracts, allowing the build plate to be viewed from the front of the printer. This is a not-so-subtle jab at Prusa’s INDX, which has tools mounted to a fixed rack that one must peer through to see the first layer printing. It adds a layer of unneeded complexity, but it also looks pretty cool in action.

The photo below shows all nine tools lowered, using a maintenance setting. Normally, only one tool lowers at a time while in use.

Unlike previous Bambu Lab 3D printers, the AMS is required, not optional. Instead of a box that can be parked here or there, the AMS 3H is designed to screw into the top of the H3C. The plastic sides have a weird organic shape, but are actually quite practical – hidden side panels pop out for maintenance, allowing you to reach into the machine. The massive lid can be propped open, which is good because it’s most likely going to be over your head.

The tools ship attached to the bottom of the AMS. Though the H3C has the same build volume, and the same length and width as the H2 line, it is doubtful that an H2 can be upgraded to an H3C by simply plopping an AMS 3H on top of an older machine.

The AMS 3H has a separate chamber with a magnetically attached lid covering slots 6 and 8, which can dry filament at up to 85°C. All filament slots except 2 and 4 can bypass the AMS feeders through a hole in the bottom to directly feed TPU to the tools. The spool slots are numbered in a zigzag method, with 2,4,6 and 8 on the back row and 1, 3, 5, 7, and 9 on the front. All the filament feeders are in the middle of the two rows, with the even-numbered spools feeding forward like always, and the odd spools feeding backward. It can get a bit complicated if you’re not using Bambu-branded filament with RFID tags. Being on the short side, I have to use a step stool to see the feeders.

The body of the Bambu Lab H3C looks similar to the rest of the H series except for the toolhead. The Flexfeed extruder can adjust automatically to push multiple filament widths and does well with some of the softest TPU I’ve tried.

The H3C has active chamber heating, up to 65°C, goes to 350°C at the hot end and 120°C on the bed, which makes printing PPA and PPS, nylons, ABS, and ASA easy. For the smellier filaments, it has active carbon and HEPA filtration.

The ventilation is sufficient to run lower-temperature filaments like PLA with the printer’s door closed.

The printer has an excellent camera that is used for AI detection on prints and can be monitored from the Bambu Handy app as well as the device page of Bambu Slicer. The camera can also capture time-lapses of prints when a USB stick is inserted in the slot behind the touch screen.

The Bambu Lab H3C has a footprint similar to the other H series printers but does require space on both sides to access the side panels of the AMS 3H. With the top-mounted AMS and the space required for the tool wiring and Bowden tubes, the Bambu Lab H3C requires a total of 1306 mm in height (over four feet) to operate comfortably. When resting on a standard-height counter or the 3ft tall racks we normally use for our printers, I needed a small ladder to reach the rear spools in the AMS 3H. Even my husband, who is 6’4,” had to stretch to reach the back. Shortly after I had the printer set up, I received the Bambu Station, which, at 23.5 inches tall on its wheels, is a great solution for keeping this printer in reach. If you don’t have a shelf or table the height of a coffee table, I recommend getting one.

Bambu is also introducing a companion E1 Air Purifier that syncs with the H3C and activates when you start a print job. We did not receive an E1 to test, and we’re not sure if the air cleaner will be offered as a standalone product. It is part of the premium $3,049 combo that includes the printer, rolling cart, and several spools of filament.

The Bambu Lab H3C arrives in two large assemblies: the main printer and all the things attached to the AMS 3H. After removing the packing material, zip ties holding the tool head in place, four screws holding the bed in place, and two retainers holding the rack in place, the AMS 3H can be hefted on top of the base unit. This is best done while the unit is on the floor or at least somewhere with a bunch of overhead clearance.

The touch screen attaches to the base unit without tools, and the ribbon cable is just long enough to reach the back of the screen. The nine tools have to be manually placed in the front rack, which takes some time until you learn how they attach. At least one needed to be attached once the printer was powered up.

The AMS 3H has its own power cord for the heater and a cable connecting it to the base. There are preinstalled screws to attach the Base unit to the AMS 3H, but I left these unused since removing the front panel requires tipping the AMS 3H back a bit.

The Bambu Lab H3C performs a bed level as part of its initial calibration suite and again before each print. This works flawlessly in practice.

When using non-TPU 1.75mm filament, the AMS 3H loads filament just like a regular AMS. Filament is inserted into the feeder, which automatically pulls it into the toolhead when that tool is selected. The RFID tag on Bambu Lab branded filament will automatically enter the filament type and color. Other filament brands need to be entered manually into the printer via the touchscreen.

The AMS 3H does not have powered rollers to retract the filament. You will need to select Unload from the filament screen, and the printer will cut the filament and retract it from the tool head. You will need to manually pull the filament out the rest of the way.

2.85mm filament and softer TPU will need to be manually fed through a port on the bottom of the AMS 3H. All filament slots except 2 and 4 have this port. The filament bypasses the tubing to feed directly into the tools. The standard Bowden tube must be removed from the tool and inserted into a holder on the side. For 1.75mm TPU, a small piece of Bowden tube is inserted into the top of the tool to help feed the filament correctly. 2.85mm filament can be inserted directly into the tool. Once inserted, filament using the bypass must be loaded manually.

Bambu Studio, based on PrusaSlicer and its forbear, is one of the best slicers out there. Working seamlessly with the printer for filament mapping, control, monitoring, and access to all of the files on Makerworld, it is a joy to use.

The H3C is a much faster machine than its predecessors because it does not need to reel filament back and forth from the AMS. However, it still uses a purge tower, which can waste a bit of time and filament. It only takes about six seconds to swap tools, which is quite remarkable.

The H3C did not come with filament samples, but Bambu Lab did send along several spools of PLA Palette and spools of 2.85 75A TPU to try. You’re going to need a bunch of filament for this beast, so check out our list of best filaments for 3D printing for suggestions.

To get the measure of the Bambu Lab H3C, I loaded up one of my favorite prints, a steampunk dragon by the designer Cinderwing 3D. I had previously printed it in eight PLA colors using two AMS modules on an Anycubic Kobra 3 Max, which is a bed slinger. That first print took 37 hours and 28 minutes and wasted 267g of filament in poop and purge.

Using a .2mm layer height and an average print speed of 140mm/s, the H3C cut that time down to a bit less than 24 hours, and only wasted 68g in its purge tower. The print quality is excellent, and all the colors came out clean. The main color is Prusa Pearl Mouse, Polymaker Panchroma Silver, and black, white, magenta, yellow, and cyan from Bambu’s new Palette PLA, which is actually designed for color mixing.

Eight colors were not enough. I swapped out the 2.85mm tool with the spare 1.75mm tool and printed a nine-color Princess Donut and Mongo from Dungeon Crawler Carl (my latest obsession). I have been using this print to test out other tool changers for a future comparison. The print came out fantastic, with super shiny silks and clean color transitions. I had to “hand paint” this in Bambu Slicer, which took a bit of time. The teeth on the print are a bit shaky, but that is due to the insane amount of support this model required.

This printed in 15 hours and 12 minutes, with a 152.6-gram prime tower, using a .2mm layer height and default settings. For comparison, the same print with the same settings but only 5 colors on the H2C took 24 hours and 44 minutes and used 111.4grams on its prime tower. The Prusa INDX took a similar amount of time (15 hours and 38 minutes) to run the same print in 8 colors, but only needed to waste 24grams of prime purge.

Princess Donut (Cat) Polymaker dual silk sunset gold-red, Polymaker Panchroma Silk Purple, Jessie Elixir Electric Lemonade.

Mongo (Dinosaur): Polymaker silk blue, Polymaker white HT-PLA Pro, Bambu pink PLA basic.

Saddle: Inland brown PLA+, Hobby Lobby PLA wood, Elegoo red silk PLA.

The Bambu Lab H3C is one of the best printers I’ve tested for printing TPU. The AMS 3H bypass ports require a tiny bit of set up, but they are much better than dealing with an external spool holder. With nine slots at my disposal, I can always have a spool of TPU ready to go.

My first TPU print was a strap hinge that combines normal 95A TPU with stiff PLA brackets. The designer wove the two filaments together manually, though you can make your own hinges flexible by using “beam interlocking” in the slicer.

I printed this in Polymaker red PLA and Polymaker red TPU 95A and the hinge is extremely strong. Printed with a .2mm layer height and running at an average speed of 115 mm/s the print completed in just over an hour.

I was excited to try the 2.85mm 75A TPU that Bambu Lab sent, and it did not disappoint. The 2.85mm nozzle comes preset to run filament directly from the bypass and just requires manual loading. This is the softest TPU I’ve ever run, and the FlexFeed extruder handled it perfectly.

I first ran the wrist rest from the printer’s memory using Bambu Lab’s green 75A TPU. The print took 50 minutes to run, used only 20g of filament, and is very squishy. I used the same filament and printed some tires for an RC project I’m working on. Using a .2mm layer height and an average print speed of 75mm/s the tires took 57 minutes per set of two. They look nice and are super squishy. They only have 2 walls and 7% concentric infill, so they are extremely lightweight.

For ABS, I ran a two-color Magic 8-ball-style D20. Dice Studio lets you print custom dice with numbers or text and export it as a 3mf file ready to print. This is printed in Ionized Cobalt Black Matte ABS and Natural Matte ABS from Fusion Filaments. The print was very nice, with just a little roughness on the overhangs on the very bottom of the die, and a little roughness on the text where it becomes very small.

To really test out the heated chamber, I used a filament I struggle with: PA6 GF. I printed a Twist Lock Container using grey PA6 GF from Sunlu. I ran it with 3 walls and a .2mm layer height at an average speed of 140mm/s. The print took nearly 6 hours to complete. The result is a nearly indestructible canister that looks good too.

The H3C is quite the multimaterial powerhouse and handles every material equally well. I don’t own any filament that it can’t print well, from soft TPU to PPS. Multiple nozzle sizes and multiple filament sizes can all be used together, and you’ve got nine places to put it all. Unless you need a print farm, I can’t really see the need for a different printer. This is a one-stop shop.

With a retail price of $2,499 for the H3C Tool Changer, this is a printer to definitely put on your wish list. In fact, I’m so impressed with the tool changer aspect, I keep forgetting that it’s also a helmet-class printer, which makes this machine capable of handling nearly any print you need to create.

If the Bambu Lab H3C seems out of reach, you can also check out last year’s Bambu Lab H2C, which is no slouch at multimaterial and has the same build volume. It’s currently on sale for $1,899 with a four-color AMS combo. The Snapmaker U1 is the most affordable tool changer we like on the market right now, and though it is smaller and only four-color, the $699 Black Friday price tag is very tempting. And of course, we have the Prusa CORE One INDX eight-color machine for those who want to keep their printers off the cloud, currently retailing at $1,949 as a kit.

Denise Bertacchi is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware US, covering 3D printing. Denise has been crafting with PCs since she discovered Print Shop had clip art on her Apple IIe. She loves reviewing 3D printers because she can mix all her passions: printing, photography, and writing.

#10420. Google confirms something was draining Pixel Watch batteries, fix rolling out

Google has confirmed that some Pixel Watches are experiencing unexpected battery drain, but it also says a fix is already available.

Last week, we reported on a trending Pixel Watch complaint – sudden and out-of-the-ordinary battery drain. More than a few posts and comments on sites like Reddit suggested Pixel Watch users were noticing an uptick in battery usage, leaving them with less than half of their normal usage.

Google reached out to 9to5Google with a statement shortly after the issue showed up, noting it was aware of the problem. Yesterday, it stated that a fix for the Pixel Watch battery drain issue is being rolled out now. The update is expected to appear for all users “over the next couple of days.”

The fix for the battery drain issue, affecting a small number of Pixel Watch users has begun rolling-out and will be complete over the next couple of days. To implement the fix, please update your Pixel Watch apps by pressing the crown on your Pixel Watch, opening the Play Store app, and tapping Manage apps > Update all.

Plummeting battery life didn’t affect every single Pixel Watch, but a number of users across models like the Pixel Watch 2 and up were more than enough to suggest something other than hardware was causing the issue.

Google hasn’t commented on where the issue originated, and whether it came from a recent Wear OS update. It’s possible it could have been one of the Pixel Watch’s proprietary apps, or something in general software that demanded too much background energy.

In any case, this software update is expected to fix any unexpected battery drain on Pixel Watch models that were affected, and should be available before your Pixel Watch can run out again.

#10416. Microsoft Authenticator is ditching its own password backup for Google One

Microsoft is ditching its own password backup option for the Microsoft Authenticator app for Android and instead using Google One for that service.

Microsoft Authenticator is a one-time password / two-factor authentication app that works more or less like Google Authenticator. The app on your phone spits out a one-time code that you’ll use for logging into various online accounts.

Starting in January, though, Microsoft Authenticator is doing away with Microsoft Account backup for your one-time password connections. Microsoft detailed the change on a support page, explaining that backup on Android will now run through Google One backup. Users will need to toggle on Microsoft Authenticator in Google One’s backup settings on their device.

Starting January 2027, you will no longer use your Microsoft personal account to enable backup. You will need to enable “Microsoft Authenticator” from Google One backup.

It’s an interesting change, to say the least, but it’s not unprecedented. Microsoft Authenticator already relied on iCloud Backup on iOS to back up one-time passwords and accounts.

The change kicks in starting in January of next year, which is mere weeks away at this point.

#10415. Building a Human Review Pipeline for AI Search: A Practical Developer’s Guide

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI search feature can return valid JSON, render correctly, and still give an unhelpful answer.

Imagine a documentation assistant answering a question about an API's current authentication requirements. It retrieves an outdated page, summarizes it fluently, and displays a citation. Every technical component appears to work, but the user receives instructions that no longer apply.

Automated checks are valuable, yet they cannot replace a clearly defined judgment about whether an answer solves the user's problem.

This guide builds a human-review workflow for a hypothetical API documentation assistant. You will define a review rubric, validate submissions with Python, measure reviewer disagreement, and turn accepted judgments into regression cases. The Python examples use Pydantic 2 and scikit-learn; they do not require a model-provider API key.

Start by identifying the failure you want to detect.

A documentation assistant usually has several stages: receive a question, retrieve relevant documents, generate an answer, and present that answer with supporting material.

Do not collapse these into a single “good answer” rating. A low score without a reason tells the team little about what to fix.

SitePoint’s introduction to retrieval-augmented generation provides background on how retrieval and generation fit together.

Preserve the exact question, answer revision, retrieved passages, document versions, and retrieval timestamp for each run. Store a snapshot manifest with stable passage IDs, source URLs, and content hashes. Reviewers should see the material supplied to that run, not today’s edited page. Keep the model and prompt configuration with the run so a later review can be traced to the behavior it evaluated.

A useful rubric defines observable criteria and separates evidence support from task completion and source suitability. Give each review the same user task and expected API version. Otherwise, reviewers may judge different questions while appearing to disagree about one answer.

For an answer about API authentication, use separate labels such as:

Supported: every material factual claim is backed by the supplied evidence.

Partially supported: some material claims lack support, while others are grounded.

Unsupported: the answer's central instruction lacks support or contradicts the evidence.

Cannot assess: the necessary evidence is missing, unreadable, or inaccessible.

Define task completion independently as complete, incomplete, or cannot assess. For source suitability, use suitable, outdated, or cannot assess. An answer can faithfully summarize an obsolete page while failing the request for current instructions.

Consider a fictional API migration: version 2 accepts X-API-Key, while version 3 requires Authorization: Bearer . If the supplied evidence contains only the version 2 page, an answer repeating X-API-Key can be supported by its evidence but still use an outdated source. If the evidence explicitly requires Bearer and the answer invents X-API-Key, label the answer unsupported. These are different engineering failures.

Also establish what reviewers should do when the source is outdated. They may correctly conclude that an answer reflects its evidence while separately flagging that evidence as unsuitable for the user's current task.

Start with a calibration batch covering clear successes, unsupported claims, missing evidence, and version mismatches. Have two reviewers label the same items independently, then compare their reasons. Revise ambiguous definitions before increasing volume; the batch is for improving the rubric, not estimating production quality.

Keep a rubric version on every review. Labels produced under different definitions should not be mixed without checking comparability.

A spreadsheet can help during exploration, but an engineering pipeline needs a stable record format.

Pydantic can validate a review record before it enters the dataset. Install Pydantic 2 and scikit-learn in your project environment, then use this model:

from pydantic import BaseModel, ConfigDict, Field, ValidationError

model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid", strict=True)

answer_revision: str = Field(min_length=1)

completion: Literal["complete", "incomplete", "cannot_assess"]

source_fit: Literal["suitable", "outdated", "cannot_assess"]

rationale: str = Field(min_length=20, max_length=2000)

Strict mode limits type coercion, and extra="forbid" rejects unexpected fields. The model checks shape and allowed values; it cannot establish that a judgment is correct. A minimum rationale length does not guarantee a useful explanation. The snapshot ID references a manifest, which can also record that retrieval produced no usable passages.

On the server, resolve the item, answer revision, snapshot, and passage IDs against the stored run. Reject IDs that do not belong together. Obtain the reviewer identity from the authenticated session rather than trusting a submitted reviewer_id. Store a server-generated submission time and preserve review revisions instead of silently overwriting earlier judgments.

Run this after the model definition. It accepts one fictional review and rejects both an unknown label and an extra field:

"rationale": "The answer invents X-API-Key; p1 requires Bearer.",

raise AssertionError("Invalid review was accepted")

The expected error types are literal_error and extra_forbidden. Add separate integration tests for mismatched evidence IDs, unauthorized submissions, and retries. Use a database uniqueness rule or an idempotency key so a repeated request does not create a second independent review.

More reviews do not automatically produce better evidence. If a task is ambiguous, scaling it can simply reproduce the ambiguity.

The workflow needs clear eligibility requirements, examples, escalation routes, and acceptance criteria. Reviewers assessing API behavior may need domain knowledge that general writing fluency does not provide.

If you work with an external AI-data contributor service such as Acquirox, define the task contract before assigning work: required expertise, permitted source material, output fields, and escalation rules. Keep ownership of the rubric, evaluation set, and acceptance decisions within the product team. Contributor capacity alone does not establish expertise in the API being reviewed.

Pilot the actual task in the review interface. Track drafts, submitted reviews, items needing adjudication, and final decisions as distinct workflow states. Reviewer judgments should remain immutable once submitted; corrections should create a new revision with a reason. Keep adjudication separate from the original labels.

For data involving users or contributors, document permitted uses, access boundaries, and retention. Remove credentials and unnecessary personal information from review material. An internal support transcript should not become a broadly accessible annotation task by accident.

Distinguish annotation from usability research. A skilled reviewer can judge whether an answer is supported, but that does not prove a first-time user can understand the interface.

SitePoint’s guide to choosing usability tests and participants offers related guidance on matching participants and methods to the question being investigated.

The review interface shapes the data it collects.

Label Studio is an option for configurable labeling interfaces and data import/export. If you use it, map exported annotations into the Review schema through an adapter; do not assume its export structure matches your application contract. A custom interface may be appropriate when reviewers need API-version navigation or passage-level citations.

The original question and relevant user context.

The evidence snapshot supplied to the model.

Separate controls for support and task completion.

A rationale field and an explicit “cannot assess” option.

Display stable passage IDs beside the evidence and let reviewers attach those IDs to their rationale. Show the source version and retrieval time near each passage. Render retrieved content as untrusted text or sanitized markup, and never treat instructions embedded in a source document as reviewer instructions. Avoid loading executable third-party content inside the review panel.

Avoid showing a preferred model name or a previous reviewer's verdict before an independent judgment. For pairwise comparisons, randomize answer order and include appropriate tie or neither-acceptable outcomes.

Design for keyboard use, visible focus, readable text, and explicit selection states. Keep evidence and the answer accessible without requiring excessive horizontal scrolling.

Start all judgment controls unselected and validate them on submission. Use labeled radio groups, an accessible error summary, and visible saving/submission status. A failed save should preserve the draft. Disable duplicate submissions in the UI, but enforce duplicate handling on the server as well.

Practical check: observe a reviewer completing several borderline examples. Record where they pause, misinterpret a label, or cannot find the necessary source material.

When two reviewers disagree, investigate before reducing their decisions to a majority vote.

Different assumptions about the user's expertise.

A genuine error by one or both reviewers.

Preserve independent labels and store the adjudicated result separately. This allows the team to study ambiguity instead of erasing it.

For two reviewers applying categorical labels to the same items, scikit-learn provides Cohen's kappa:

from sklearn.metrics import cohen_kappa_score

("q1", "a1", "s1", "auth-v1"): "supported",

("q2", "a1", "s2", "auth-v1"): "unsupported",

("q3", "a1", "s3", "auth-v1"): "supported",

("q4", "a1", "s4", "auth-v1"): "partially_supported"

("q3", "a1", "s3", "auth-v1"): "partially_supported"

raise ValueError("Review sets must contain identical items")

raw_agreement = sum(x == y for x, y in zip(left, right)) / len(keys)

print(f"Raw agreement: {raw_agreement:.2f}")

print(f"Kappa: {cohen_kappa_score(left, right):.2f}")

This toy example prints raw agreement of 0.75 and kappa of about 0.64. Four items are not enough to assess a review program. In production, check for duplicate reviews before building dictionaries, use one rubric version per comparison, report unmatched items, and align all records by item, answer revision, and evidence snapshot.

Kappa measures agreement beyond chance under its assumptions, not correctness. Inspect raw agreement, label frequencies, and the disagreement table alongside it. A single-category batch can make kappa undefined; report that limitation rather than substituting a reassuring score. Treat cannot_assess explicitly and report its frequency instead of silently excluding difficult cases.

If both reviewers apply the same flawed rubric consistently, agreement can be high while the evaluation remains unhelpful.

Once difficult cases are adjudicated, turn them into regression cases with explicit acceptance criteria. For the fictional authentication task: use version 3 evidence, cite the passage requiring Bearer, and do not invent a header. If version 3 evidence is missing, an acceptable response should explain the gap rather than provide confident instructions.

Expected behavior or acceptance criteria.

DVC can track larger datasets alongside Git. In an existing Git repository with DVC initialized and data/reviewed-auth.jsonl present, a minimal versioning step is:

git add data/reviewed-auth.jsonl.dvc data/.gitignore

git commit -m "Version authentication review cases"

This commits metadata, not the dataset bytes. Configure an access-controlled DVC remote and push the data before expecting another machine or CI runner to retrieve it. Keep credentials and sensitive review material out of public repositories.

Keep development examples separate from held-out evaluation cases. If developers repeatedly tune prompts against the same questions, those questions become less useful as evidence of generalization.

Split related examples together where appropriate. Near-duplicate questions from one support conversation should not be scattered across development and evaluation sets.

Use two evaluation passes. First, hold the evidence snapshot fixed to compare answer-generation changes. Then test retrieval against the intended document versions to detect stale or irrelevant sources. Record per-item outcomes and report failures by category; an overall improvement can hide a regression in version-specific or multilingual questions.

SitePoint’s article on testing nondeterministic AI behavior provides further context for repeatable evaluation workflows.

A correct answer can still fail in the interface.

Users may overlook a caveat, misunderstand whether an operation has been performed, or miss that the evidence applies to an older API version.

Pair answer review with practical usability tasks:

Find the authentication requirements for version 3 and identify the source that supports them.

Observe whether users complete the task and whether their confidence matches the available evidence.

SitePoint’s overview of user research methods helps distinguish task observation from preference surveys.

Turn interface failures into browser tests: a citation opens the expected document version, its passage ID matches the displayed evidence, missing evidence offers a recovery action, and a failed review submission preserves the draft. Verify that reviewers cannot access unassigned restricted items by changing an ID in the URL. These checks complement human judgments; they do not replace them.

Do not use a fluent response as a substitute for a clear interaction. The interface should distinguish retrieved information, generated interpretation, and actions the application has actually completed.

Start with one authentication task, a versioned rubric, and a small calibration batch. Use Pydantic for ingestion checks, Label Studio or a custom interface for review, scikit-learn for agreement analysis, and Git/DVC for dataset provenance. Add each tool only when it solves a concrete workflow need.

At release time, run deterministic checks for schema, evidence references, permissions, and interface behavior. Compare candidate answers against adjudicated acceptance criteria and send new ambiguous failures back for review. Record the dataset revision and configurations with the results so another developer can reproduce the comparison.

The purpose of human review is to produce evidence the team can act on. A smaller collection of well-defined, traceable judgments is often more useful than a large pile of ratings whose meaning nobody can explain.

#10414. Hands-on with the DockCase Mount Ling 2 Pro
