Mount Ling 2 Pro offers a lot of flexibility for a gaming dock.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

DockCase is no stranger to Kickstarting unique SSD enclosures and docks, with the company’s Visual Smart M.2 enclosure making its way onto our list of the best SSD enclosures. The Mount Ling 2 Pro, however, is gunning for a spot among the best USB-C docks. It’s a dock for handhelds like the Steam Deck and Switch 2, an external enclosure for an NVMe SSD, and a game capture device, all packaged up in a dock that can show off your handheld and expand your port selection.

There are two versions of the dock: the standard Mount Ling 2 and the Pro version. The only difference between them is the internal capture card. The base version is $109 via an early bird deal while the Kickstarter gets underway, and the Pro version is $149. They’ll be priced at $199 and $249, respectively, after the early deals are gone.

It’s the world’s first “smart creator dock,” says DockCase, which is partially true. There are other docks that give you additional inputs, a stand, and capture capabilities with a handheld, and at a lower price. The AVerMedia X’TRA GO, for instance, offers all of that for $160. The differentiator here is the internal M.2 slot that can support up to 4TB of NVMe storage, with points to mount 2280, 2242, and 2230-sized drives. Of course, this external storage only works with platforms that normally support external storage (so, not the Switch or Switch 2).

For PC-adjacent handhelds running Windows or Linux, the Mount Ling 2 Pro is an expensive but useful dock that gives you capture, storage, and port expandability in one attractive package. For the Switch and Switch 2, the usefulness isn’t as clear. You can’t use the SSD with either platform, and Nintendo’s game of whack-a-mole with third-party accessories means a system update could break HDMI passthrough (and, in the process, the capture function of the dock).

DockCase says the Mount Ling 2 Pro supports the Steam Deck, ROG Ally, Switch, Switch 2, and “more,” presumably wrapping up other Windows- or Linux-based handhelds like the ROG Ally X Xbox or Lenovo Legion Go S. Switch and Switch 2 are supported, as well, though getting Nintendo’s handheld to connect properly isn’t always smooth.

The dock connects to your handheld with a USB 3.2 Gen Type-C connection capable of 10 Gbps, and DockCase includes a small, braided 40 Gbps cable in the box, along with two right-angle connectors. That single connection gives you up to 100W through the USB-C port, along with Gigabit Ethernet, an HDMI 2.0 connection (4K@60Hz), a single USB 3.2 Gen 2 Type-A port, and a USB 2.0 port. An additional USB-C connection serves as another USB 3.2 Gen 2 port on the Mount Ling 2, while it becomes the capture card output on the Pro model.

Interestingly, there’s also an optical audio output available. Although I don’t suspect most users will put it to use (HDMI transfers audio, as well), it’s a nice fallback if you plan on using the dock with a more elaborate audio setup. It’s a nice bonus, though I would’ve liked a 3.5mm audio jack as well, considering handhelds no longer have access to that connection.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The dock itself is constructed almost entirely of plastic, and holding it in your hands, you can tell it’s filled with more air than hardware. It’s a sizable package at 8.4 x 7 x 3.5 inches. Outside of the shell, there’s a thick layer of firm silicone to rest your handheld on. There are grooves to provide a natural resting place for just about any handheld, as well as a deep central cut that fits the Switch and Switch 2 specifically.

You can install your SSD at the bottom of the dock, with mounting points for 2280, 2242, and 2230 drives. There's a single 40mm fan internally, as well as venting around the back and side edges, to keep the drive cool. The fan will automatically adjust its speed out of the box, but you’re free to manually adjust the speed or turn the fan off, if you want.

Unfortunately, there’s no room for a heatsink. The face of the drive sits nearly flush against the vented cover on the bottom of the dock. Even if you left the cover off, most heatsinks would push past the rubber feet and prop the dock up.

Setting up the Mount Ling 2 Pro is mostly a matter of plugging everything in and getting on your way. The dock goes through a connecting phase when you hook up a new device, and it’s finicky. Sometimes it would drop one part of the connection, like charging, while picking up another, particularly with the Switch 2. You want to have the dock fully connected before hooking up your host.

The main page of the dock’s screen provides HDMI, SSD, and power info at a glance, which is helpful to quickly check, for example, that the HDMI passthrough is operating at the correct resolution and refresh rate without digging into the settings. A swipe to the right brings up five quick-access settings, which just take you into the settings menu, while a swipe to the right gives you access to volume and fan controls. The volume adjustment on the dock is passed through to the host.

Swiping up instead gives you the full settings menu, which has more to monitor than adjust. You can see the SSD details (including SMART monitors), the status of USB connections, and your HDMI info, andother details about the dock. For settings, there are different HDMI transfer modes, in case you run into a broken handshake, along with power delivery settings if you need to roll back to PD 2.0.

For the dock itself, you have some small control over the fan and built-in lighting. For the fan, you can adjust the speed manually, turn it off, or leave it on the default automatic mode. The lighting settings are surprisingly bare. You can choose between blue, red, or a gradient between them, and that’s it. Otherwise, you can just dim or turn off the lights. If you find the screen and lights distracting, there’s a dedicated button on the back of the dock to turn all the lights off (including the screen).

Although the screen is responsive with minimal lag between swipes and taps, the software running here isn’t perfect. Particularly with the Switch 2, if I connected things in the wrong order, the dock would force itself to reboot, only sometimes picking up the Switch 2 when it came back. DockCase offers firmware updates over USB, and hopefully it gets a patch between units shipped to fix some of these quirks.

The firmware updates are a nice sign, but they bring up an unfortunate reality of creating a device for the Switch 2 without Nintendo’s seal of approval. Both the Switch and Switch 2 are infamously fickle with third-party docks and hubs. A system update could break compatibility, forcing a firmware update.

DockCase plays up Switch 2 support on its product page, and that is a big draw. However, the dock can’t get around the limitations that Nintendo itself has placed on its handheld. You may as well fill the SSD slot with concrete if you’re docking a Switch 2, and docking the device is troublesome. It took multiple emails for me to get the Switch 2 to pass through its HDMI signal at all. You have to connect everything in a specific way, and critically, have the Switch 2 already unlocked when you plug it into the bottom USB-C port; the top port won’t work.

With the Steam Deck, everything was seamless, and although I didn’t have a Windows handheld on hand to try out, I don’t anticipate there will be major issues. DockCase may offer a lot of functionality in a single box with the Mount Ling Pro 2, but it’s all operating over standard connections that are well supported in the Windows ecosystem.

You can use the SSD with the Steam Deck, though you’ll need to head to desktop mode to partition the drive and add a folder for Steam games. The process is similar on Windows handhelds if you’re using some sort of handheld-native overlay.

For the capture portion, it serves its purpose. Critically, DockCase doesn’t have any software for capturing or recording like the mainstream capture cards you’ll find from Elgato and AVerMedia, so you’ll need to use something else like OBS. For any of these types of recording/streaming platforms, the DockCase shows up as a simple video capture device, with resolution and frame rate determined by your settings on the host device (assuming it’s within that 4K60 limit that HDMI 2.0 is capable of).

There isn’t much of a need here for HDMI 2.1, considering the types of devices connecting to the Mount Ling Pro 2, though it would’ve been a nice upside. You can still push the refresh rate higher, just not at 4K. And in fairness to DockCase, the devices it’s targeting really have no business driving a 4K display.

You shouldn’t expect wonders out of SSD performance with the Mount Ling 2 Pro, given that it uses a USB 3.2 Gen 2 connection that’s only capable of up to 10 Gbps. High performance in an external enclosure is the game of Thunderbolt 5 docks with M.2 storage, which can often reach sequential reads above 5,000 MB/s over the 80 Gbps Thunderbolt 5 interface. Here, the connection will bottleneck your speeds, all the way down to typical PCIe 3.0 NVMe SSDs.

Still, in the context of gaming on a handheld, that isn’t the end of the world. It’s nice to have that much storage on tap for a device like the Steam Deck, which is otherwise only expandable with a larger internal SSmicroSDicro SD card.

In CrystalDiskMark, the Mount Ling 2 Pro landed with sequential reads and writes around 820 MB/s, with random performance far lower. We tested with a 2 TB WD_Black SN770, which is a PCIe 4.0 drive that boasts sequential reads up to 5150 MB/s when installed directly in a motherboard. In the Mount Ling 2 Pro, it behaves more like an external SSD like the SK hynix Beetle X31 2TB, naturally hamstrung by the connection.

The Mount Ling 2 Pro does a lot, combining external storage, a capture card, and an attractive dock into a single package that works with both PC handhelds and the Nintendo Switch 2. The SSD performance isn’t great, as is to be expected out of a dock that uses a 10 Gbps USB link for connection, but that downside isn’t much of a concern considering the devices this dock targets.

Although Switch 2 support is a big upside, it’s also fickle. You don’t get access to the SSD, and the connection can fail if you don’t follow the order of operations for connection. The Mount Ling 2 Pro makes sense if you have a Switch 2 and a PC handheld of some kind, but as a pure Switch 2 dock, it doesn’t make much sense. You’d be better off buying an external capture card for use with the official dock.

Jake Roach is the Senior CPU Analyst at Tom’s Hardware, writing reviews, news, and features about the latest consumer and workstation processors.

#10411. Xbox’s smallest-ever controller is surprisingly comfortable

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Nano controller is designed for young people, anyone with smaller hands, and gaming on the go.

The Nano controller is designed for young people, anyone with smaller hands, and gaming on the go.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Even though I knew Microsoft’s new Xbox Nano controller was tiny, I still gasped when I unboxed it a few days ago. The compact controller is small enough to slip into a pocket unnoticed, and it has been designed specifically for younger people, or for anyone with smaller hands.

I’m not young, but I do have small hands, and I’ve been surprised at how comfortable the Nano is during gaming sessions. At just over 5 inches long, almost everything on this controller has been shrunk down somehow. The ABXY buttons are ever so slightly smaller than a regular Xbox controller, and the thumbsticks feel like miniature versions. Microsoft hasn’t changed the D-pad, though, or the size of the glowing Xbox button at the center of the controller.

The big difference I noticed with the Nano over a regular Xbox controller was the size of the triggers. While they are fully analog and pressure sensitive, they have more of a click when you bottom them out compared with the thud on a bigger Xbox controller. They’re noticeably smaller, as you’d expect, and the bumper buttons above the triggers feel less clicky than the regular ones.

I thought the Nano controller would feel awkward after a few hours of gaming, but the grips really help with comfort levels — a lot of smaller controllers sacrifice grips to keep the controller thin and lightweight. It does mean the Nano doesn’t sit flush on a table, but at 101 grams (0.22 pounds) it’s still really lightweight.

I can’t speak for those with bigger hands, but I’d expect the Nano controller won’t be well-suited to everyone. The size makes it ideal for young children, though. I tried to get my 1-year-old son to try the Nano, and he responded with a smile, so I guess he likes it.

I haven’t had enough time to get a full idea of battery life yet, but it seems to drain around 10 percent in around 30 to 40 minutes. That suggests a lot less battery life than the 30 hours you’d get on a rechargeable pack for the larger Xbox wireless controller. Thankfully, the Nano charges quickly, and I found I could get from 30 percent to 60 percent charge in less than 10 minutes. You can plug in a USB-C charger to top up the battery and carry on playing while you charge.

The only thing missing on the Nano controller that I would have loved is vibration support. I assume it’s not there because of an impact on battery life, because even the smaller Nintendo Joy-Con controllers have rumble support.

The Xbox Nano controller will be available on November 4th, priced at $49.99.

AI demand is tightening DRAM and NAND supply, pushing up costs for device makers and leaving less memory available for consumer electronics

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Apple is about to feel the squeeze from rising memory prices, with the cost of RAM climbing just as the company heads into its busiest shopping season. According to Reuters, Apple expects to pay more for memory in the December quarter, with the CEO, John Ternus, describing the current price increases as “unprecedented. The company has so far managed to soften the blow by using memory bought at lower prices, cutting costs elsewhere and raising prices on some products. However, it is reported that its manufacturing bill has climbed by $33 billion in just a few months.

But that cushion is getting thinner, and Apple expects memory purchases to become even more expensive into 2027. “What we're seeing with memory pricing right now is unprecedented. And as we said earlier in the September quarter, we did see higher memory costs as compared to the June quarter, which were partly offset by the benefit of carrying inventory and lower costs on certain non-memory components. Now for the December quarter, we expect even higher memory costs, partly offset by a few factors. So as you know, we changed the price on many of our products, including Mac and iPad at the end of June and iPhone in September, and these price increases provided some offset.” Ternus said during Apple’s November 2 earnings call.

Memory is used across Apple's hardware lineup, so sustained price increases could affect manufacturing cost for several product categories—although Apple has not announced another round of price increases. Absorbing higher components could, however, indefinitely become difficult. Dave Nicholson, Chief Research Officer at the Futurum Group, also identified memory prices as a growing pressure point for Apple's margins and hardware pricing through the holiday season and into 2027. According to him, Apple is in a different position from companies pouring hundreds of billions of dollars into AI data center infrastructure. The impact of higher component costs is easier to quantify than the eventual returns on massive AI capital expenditure, he said.

The next pressure point for @Apple may not be AI CapEx, but the rising cost of memory across its hardware portfolio. @TheFuturumGroup Chief Research Officer @DaveN007 explained that higher memory prices could put more pressure on Apple’s margins and pricing through the holiday season and into 2027. Even with demand holding up, the question is how much of those higher costs consumers will absorb. Dave also sees Apple in a stronger position than companies committing hundreds of billions to data center infrastructure, because the impact of higher component costs is easier to quantify than the long-term returns on massive AI CapEx. ▶️ Watch Dave’s analysis of Apple’s margin outlook, memory costs, and why its AI infrastructure strategy could give the company more flexibility. #Apple #AI #Semiconductors #FuturumNovember 3, 2026

And the warnings from Apple's suppliers suggest that the cost pressure is unlikely to ease quickly. Last month, Samsung, a major supplier of RAM(DRAM), NAND flash storage and OLED displays for Apple, said that memory demand would far exceed supply as AI infrastructure continues to expand. Its memory division also reported a quarter-on-quarter increase in DRAM average prices during the third quarter, while NAND prices rose by high-teen percentages. Senior executive vice president of its memory business warned the supply crunch could extend to 2028 and beyond. Elsewhere, SK Hynix delivered a similar warning, saying it had secured long-term agreements covering more than Half of its production capacity for2027 and 2028. Micron, the third major supplier for Apple aside from Samsung and SK Hynix, is also benefiting from the surging demand for memory. Its latest published outlook warns that supply shortages in memory and storage could take “considerable time” to improve.

We have previously reported several signs of the pressure now confronting Apple. In January, the company was already scrambling to secure memory supplies amid strong demand, with then CEO Tim Cook warning that rising prices could have a greater impact on earnings. By June, rising component expenses had translated into higher retail prices. Apple increased prices across several Mac and iPad models, including a $400 increase to the starting price of the MacBook Pro, due to the RAM crisis. In July, Cook described the memory market as a “hundred-year-flood” and said Apple expected to pay more for memory in the September quarter. The company had also increased its inventory to $11.09 billion as it built a buffer against supply and price pressures.

#10407. Android Auto 18.1 update rolling out with additional prep for light mode and redesign

Google is rolling out Android Auto 18.1 to the masses and, while there are no major changes, the update continues to work on upcoming changes.

Following Android Auto 18.0’s release in mid-October, Google released v18.1 to the beta track in late October. As of this week, the update is now rolling out to stable users. As far as we’ve been able to tell thus far, there are no user-facing updates here, but there are a few things going on behind the scenes.

About APK Insight: In this “APK Insight” post, we’ve decompiled the latest version of an application that Google uploaded to the Play Store. When we decompile these files (called APKs, in the case of Android apps), we’re able to see various lines of code within that hint at possible future features. Keep in mind that Google may or may not ever ship these features, and our interpretation of what they are may be imperfect. We’ll try to enable those that are closer to being finished, however, to show you how they’ll look in case that they do ship. With that in mind, read on.

In a teardown of the latest Android Auto 18.1 update, we’ll start off by saying there’s nothing groundbreaking going on here. There’s a growing trend of making a really big deal out of any little Android Auto change or feature lately, but we’ll say right up front that there’s nothing major happening in this update.

Rather, Google continues to build out its redesign and its light mode, which at this point seems to be arriving with that redesign.

Strings that caught our eye in this update include assets specific to Samsung devices that include “dark_mode” and “light_mode” variants, though these seem to be applicable to non-Samsung devices as well. Android Auto already makes UI tweaks when you’re using a Samsung phone versus a Pixel or other Android device, so these assets are likely just being built out for the upcoming redesign. Specifically, we believe these are being prepared for Android Auto’s upcoming Quick Settings tiles which will presumably include some Samsung-specific icons or designs.

“Cielo,” the apparent codename for Android Auto’s widget-filled redesign, continues to appear in updates. In v18.1, we’re seeing mentions of map scaling and apparent animations used for the dashboard “slide in” and “slide out” effects. These aren’t a surprise as we saw them in early hands-on demos back in May.

There’s still no sign of exactly when Android Auto’s “Cielo” redesign may go live, but Google is clearly building everything out behind the scenes, implying it may come sooner than later.

#10402. How to Add AI Content Detection to Your Node.js App

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sooner or later someone on your team asks it. Maybe it’s a product manager, maybe the head of content, maybe a teacher who’s just marked forty near-identical essays: can we tell when people are pasting in ChatGPT output?

The short answer is no, not with certainty. Anyone who promises you that is selling something.

But you can get a useful signal, and you can show it to the people who actually make the call: editors, teachers, moderators. In this tutorial you’ll build a small Express app that takes some text, sends it to an AI detector API, waits for the result, and highlights the sentences that look machine-written.

I’m using the Quetext AI Detector API here. It scores each sentence, not just the whole document, and you can try it without a subscription. Most detection APIs follow the same submit, poll, fetch pattern, though. If you switch providers later, you’ll rewrite one file.

Put the key in an environment variable. Don’t hard-code it, and don’t let it anywhere near the browser.

You’ll make three calls. Analysis takes a few seconds, so the API hands you a ticket and makes you come back for the result. Keep the Quetext API docs open in another tab while you follow along; they list every endpoint, field and error code.

Every request sends your key in an X-API-Key header. The minimum input is 50 words, which sounds annoying until you realise no detector can say anything useful about a two-line comment.

And remember this number: 10 requests every 5 seconds, per account. Progress checks count. It’ll come back to bite you in the production section if you ignore it.

Create quetext.js. I like keeping everything provider-specific in one file, so the rest of the app has no idea who’s doing the detecting.

Three decisions in there that are worth a sentence each:

The errors keep their HTTP status code. A 402 means you’ve run out of credit and a 429 means you’re polling too fast, and you’ll want to handle those differently from “something broke.”

It polls every 2.5 seconds because Quetext’s docs suggest 2 to 3. Polling faster won’t speed the report up. You’ll just burn through the rate limit.

And there’s a hard timeout. Leave it out and one stuck report can hang a request forever. That’s a miserable bug to track down at 11pm.

Next, server.js. It checks the input, runs the three calls, and gives the frontend a simple response that doesn’t mention Quetext anywhere.

Look at the mapping at the end. generated_prob becomes probability, and ai_score comes back as a string like "82.50", so it gets parsed into a number. It’s a small detail. But it means your frontend never learns which provider’s field names it’s dealing with, and that’s what makes swapping providers a one-file change.

Show someone “64% AI” and they’ll jump to a conclusion. Show them a paragraph with three sentences shaded pink and they’ll actually read it.

So the page shows every sentence, coloured by how likely it is to be AI-generated. Create public/index.html:

Run node server.js and open http://localhost:3000. Try two samples: something you wrote yourself, then a raw chatbot answer on the same topic. Seeing which sentences light up teaches you more about how these detectors work than any diagram I could draw.

A quick security point. Those sentences came from your users, then went through an outside service. Treat them as untrusted. That’s why the code uses textContent and never innerHTML, as the OWASP XSS prevention cheat sheet recommends.

The demo keeps an HTTP request open while it polls. Fine on your laptop. Not fine with real traffic.

Submit the text, save the report id with a pending status, and respond straight away. A worker (BullMQ, a cron job, a serverless queue, whatever you already use) does the polling and updates the record once it’s done. The UI says “Checking…” until the result shows up.

Here’s how it goes wrong. It’s Monday morning, 20 editors open their review queues and click “Check” at roughly the same time. Each request polls on its own, and within a few seconds you’ve hit 429s across the board, because the 10-per-5-seconds limit covers your whole account, not each user.

Use one worker that loops through every pending report, with a short pause between calls. Problem solved.

You pay per word, so don’t check every blog comment. Article submissions, assignments and paid freelance work usually justify the cost. Cache results against a hash of the text too. Someone who submits the same draft three times shouldn’t cost you three times.

AI detection doesn’t catch copying. Someone can lift a 2019 blog post word for word and it’ll score 0% AI, because a human wrote it. Just not this human. If originality matters to your product, the same API has plagiarism endpoints that work exactly the same way. The API documentation covers both.

Developers skip this part. It’s also the part that ends up in angry support tickets.

In 2023, Liang and colleagues at Stanford ran essays written by non-native English speakers (all human, written for the TOEFL) through seven AI detectors. On average, the detectors labelled more than 61% of them as AI-generated. Simple vocabulary and textbook grammar read as “predictable,” and predictability is exactly what detectors look for.

Picture an international student getting an automatic zero because a script said so. You don’t want to be the person who built that.

Do that and the detector is a fast first pass that points people at what’s worth a closer look. Your humans still make the call.

You send text, the service checks how predictable each sentence would be to a language model, and you get back a probability that a machine wrote it. Most of these APIs are asynchronous. You submit, get a report ID, poll until it’s ready, then fetch the result. The better ones score individual sentences as well as the whole text, which makes building a sensible review screen much easier.

More than you’d think. Quetext’s API won’t accept fewer than 50 words, and in practice scores settle down once you’re past about 300. A product title or a one-line comment just doesn’t give a detector enough to go on. For short content, either group related pieces together or don’t bother checking it.

No. Detectors make mistakes, and research shows they flag non-native English writers far more often than native speakers. Use the score to decide what a human looks at first, not to block anything. Give reviewers the highlighted sentences, give users a way to explain themselves, and log what you decided. Your users get treated fairly and your support team avoids a pile of angry tickets.

Poll at the speed the provider recommends (every 2 to 3 seconds for Quetext), and remember that those polls count against your limit too. Once you’re in production, move checks into a background queue and let one worker poll every pending report, rather than having each user request poll by itself. When you do get a 429, wait a moment and retry.

No. If the browser calls the API directly, your key ends up in the frontend code where anyone can copy it and spend your credit. Keep the key in an environment variable on your server and send requests through your own endpoint, like the one in this tutorial. That also gives you somewhere to check input length, apply per-user limits and cache results before you pay for anything.

Carbon dioxide-spilling converted turbines have been installed in a Mississippi xAI data center despite the known health risks.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Elon Musk's xAI is using eight converted jet engine turbines to power a Mississippi data center. While the generators have the required state-level permission and permits, there remain concerns about the impact they could have on the local environment. The permits allow for a 25% higher limit in pollutants known as volatile organic compounds, but those compounds, paired with the expulsion of carbon monoxide, have environmentalists warning that the use of jet engines to power data centers is a step in the wrong direction. The New York Times reports that the Southern Environmental Law Center has already challenged Mississippi's decision to approve the turbines for use at xAI's data center.

The group argues that xAI has failed to provide guarantees that the jet-powered turbines meet emissions standards for specific pollutants, including formaldehyde. It also accuses xAI of what the report terms the "undercounting" of projected emissions in relation to the data center's power sources. This comes after the same group sued xAI over its use of gas generators without the required permits in an area with more than 100,000 inhabitants.

The generators used by xAI are converted by ProEnergy, a Missouri-based outfit that repurposes the unused General Electric turbines that are typically found attached to Boeing airplanes. The company was quick to turn its attention to the possibility of using the jet engines to power data centers after it was identified that the demand for energy was outpacing the ability of traditional utility companies to keep up. Each turbine is capable of producing up to 50 megawatts of electricity. Normally, that would be enough to keep around 15,000 homes up and running.

The availability of energy has long been an issue for data centers, both existing and those still on the drawing board. A July 2026 report predicted that data centers would use 20% of all U.S. power by 2035, a figure that was a huge 83% increase over the same forecast that was made seven months prior. Despite that news, as of April this year, half of data center buildouts had been canceled or delayed due to, among other things, a lack of power. This is an issue for xAI especially, with Musk saying in August that xAI would increase its data center capacity by 7x before the end of 2027. Delays and cancelations have the potential to derail those plans.

Public sentiment around data centers has turned sour in recent months amid power issues and concerns over how much water these facilities use. While using converted jet engines could potentially help with one issue, it seems unlikely that the pollution they generate will help assuage the concerns of locals anytime soon.

#10399. Indie games are missing launch dates because Steam is backed up

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

One developer has been waiting for weeks on approval for a game it planned to release today.

One developer has been waiting for weeks on approval for a game it planned to release today.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Many indie game developers have recently posted on social media about unexpectedly long approval wait times for their game submissions on Steam, with some of the approvals causing developers to miss their planned launch dates.

For example, Studio Daimon, the team behind an upcoming title called Food Devils, had planned to launch the game in early access today after submitting its build on October 12th. But the game is “still awaiting approval,” according to a post from game director Alfred Kamon. “Even now, we don’t know when Steam will give us the final OK to finally release our game. We sent another ticket last week saying that the situation was urgent and that we might miss our release date because of this, but it also got ignored.”

In October, publisher 2 Left Thumbs said it missed its planned launch of Wheel Make It “by nearly two weeks” due to the long wait for build reviews. You can read some details about the studio’s setbacks, including failing a review for a “one single option in the menu” not being accessible with a controller, in a Steam post. There are lots of other recent posts about review build waits as well.

Valve says on a Steam developer website that a review of a game’s store presence “typically takes 3-5 business days to complete,” though the company suggests submitting a page for review “at least 7 business days before you want it live to account for potential changes you’ll need to make.”

However, as of late, Steam has seemingly been grappling with a sharp increase in games; October’s Steam Next Fest had 7,240 games, a 65.2 percent increase over June’s Steam Next Fest, according to How to Market a Game. A Reddit user also found that 49.9 percent of all games released since Valve started asking developers in 2024 to label games that use AI now have an AI disclosure.

Kamon and 2 Left Thumbs suspect the longer waits are due to “slop” slowing down the process. “We can only assume it’s because Steam’s review queue is clogged up by AI slop, but the whole process is now taking several weeks for some developers,” said Kamon.

In a new post from Valve published after we first ran this story, the company acknowledged that following a “a significant increase in submissions for store and page review in recent months,” it’s “running a little behind right now” and taking “up to 12 business days” for reviews, saying that much of the delay is “having to re-review games.” It has updated its release checklist for developers, including requiring them to run a game through Steam for at least five minutes. Valve also says that further improvements are on the way for the build review process.

Itch.io has also been dealing with a rise in games, with the platform saying last weekend that “The number of game pages being created per-day has doubled in the past 2 months.” Last night, the platform said it “Just deleted a couple terabytes of ai slop 😌”

Update, November 3rd: Added new post from Valve.

#10397. ‘Cyclon’ uses an old Google Pixel to let AI do literally everything for you – who wants this?

As the AI era continues to find its footing in the consumer market, “Cyclon” is a new smartphone/software experience that, to start, is reusing old Google Pixel phones with a software experience that literally uses AI to do every little thing. And my biggest question is just… who actually wants this?

The pitch of Cyclon, the founders explain in a video on Twitter/X, is to “[build] an AI phone to replace the iPhone.” And the central experience seems to be… talking to a chatbot. Examples in the video include telling the AI to “handle my notifications” and letting it monitor a WhatsApp message and reply to a friend asking for photos by grabbing those photos from your gallery without you as the user needing to touch the device.

Other examples on the company’s website have mixed utility at a glance. “Order my usual on DoorDash” and calling a courier to let them into a building certainly could be useful, but “tell the group chat I’m running ten minutes late” only to have the AI send the message on WhatsApp “in your words” not only seems like a waste of time, but a pointless disconnect from reality (as is all too common with these AI ventures).

The one thing that caught my attention here is building apps on demand and being able to share them. The example given is a language learning app tailored to a specific vacation. It’s a really cool idea, though one I would imagine will have some rocky edges. Google is on a similar track with Gemini Intelligence’s “Create my widget” feature (on that note, when is that releasing on phones Google?), and I’d imagine Gemini will be able to do the same thing in time.

The actual hardware side of things is also… interesting.

Cyclon says that it has a working prototype of the idea running on Google Pixel 8a units, and it’ll ship “first-batch” backers one of those units with their $199 pre-order of a later “Cyclon Phone” that itself is expected to cost $699 – so you’re getting two phones for $699. That alone seems a little questionable in the midst of RAMageddon, but I digress.

That “final” phone hardware… sure is something.

There are some good ideas here, some big promises, but more than anything I just want to know who actually wants to use a phone like this.

I’m all for the idea of AI making my life a little easier (despite finding hardly any ways it can do that so far), but sending everything through a chatbot that does everything in the background and even generates “your words” for responses to my friends and family just sounds awful. And the state of social media isn’t exactly helping matters here. Twitter/X is 90% AI replies for engagement for this sort of thing, and Cyclon is paying influencers to boost that engagement too, so genuine reactions just seem impossible to find.

Sound off in the comments below if you’re an actual human with an opinion on this one, because I’m dying to know what real people think of this.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Intel Foundry last week made available its Intel 14A process design kit (PDK) version 0.9, an important milestone in the development of this next-generation manufacturing technology. The company says that the production node is reaching performance and yield maturity expected at this stage of development, and the results are backed by silicon validation. Intel plans to release PDK 1.0 in the first quarter of 2027, which will validate the technology's readiness for high-volume manufacturing (HVM).

A process design kit contains transistor models, design rules, and other information that enables chip designers to develop products for a particular manufacturing process using electronic design automation (EDA) tools. The accuracy and quality of the PDK are crucial for modelling how chips will perform once manufactured, and the release of PDK 0.9 is particularly important because it provides customers with a representation of Intel 14A based on actual silicon measurements rather than on theoretical models or simulations. At this stage, customers can proceed with product development with greater confidence that the technology will meet their requirements when it comes to performance and power consumption.

Intel says that PDK 0.9 lets customers develop designs intended to enter risk production around the time the process is certified, which — provided that PDK accurately reflects the characteristics of manufactured silicon — in many cases enables chip developers to greatly reduce the number of silicon revisions between initial tapeout and mass production. Of course, availability of PDK 0.9 does not indicate that Intel 14A is ready or even close to mass production, as the company expects to validate HVM readiness only with PDK 1.0, sometime in Q1 2027. Again, this does not mean that 14A will enter mass production in early 2027, as Intel itself expects to begin mass production using its 14A process technology in 2028, following risk production for its own products in the second half of 2027.

"From a customer perspective, the PDK 0.9 gives customers the confidence to design products on Intel 14A, as the technology is reaching the industry-standard performance and yield maturity expected at this stage of development, supported by silicon-backed validation," said Ben Sell, corporate vice president and general manager of front-end technology development at Intel Foundry.

Intel 14A builds on top of architectural foundations introduced with Intel 18A but incorporates several substantial enhancements. These include 2nd Generation RibbonFET 2 gate-all-around (GAA) transistors, PowerDirect backside power delivery (which delivers power right to the transistor source/drain regions without using intermediate connections like TSVs used by PowerVia), and Turbo Cells (high-performance standard cells designed to accelerate timing-critical paths in CPUs and GPUs), which provide chip designers with additional ways to increase performance and optimize power efficiency. Since Intel has previously outlined advantages of these technologies, the latest announcement does not provide updates regarding transistor performance, power consumption, or transistor density figures for Intel 14A.

Beyond improvements to transistor, power delivery, and interconnect technologies, Intel is applying manufacturing experience gained during 12 months of Intel 18A production to accelerate maturation of 14A. Specifically, the company says it has increased investments in inline defect detection, modeling, and simulation to identify potential manufacturing problems earlier in the development cycle. These initiatives are intended to improve manufacturing yields, process variability, ultimately transistor performance, and reduce production cycle times. Intel also says it has introduced several strategic initiatives to accelerate technology development, although it does not quantify their impact.

Another important change concerns Intel's relationships with equipment and materials suppliers. The company says it has revised its collaboration model to involve technology development teams and external suppliers earlier in the process development cycle. The goal of such close collaborations is to address manufacturing defects and improve equipment capabilities before they become obstacles for yield improvements after the technology enters mass production. Intel also conducts regular yield summit meetings with suppliers to accelerate progress on critical development objectives.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

"This has required evolving our collaboration model with equipment and material suppliers, and earlier engagement from our [technology development] leaders with suppliers to drive improvements in defects and equipment capabilities," Sell said.

A bonus of such early collaboration is that equipment and materials suppliers may incorporate Intel's requirements into their standard products and development roadmaps. This could give Intel an advantage over competitors, which would have to either adapt their manufacturing processes to equipment optimized around Intel's requirements or work with suppliers to develop alternative solutions from scratch.

Historically, Intel frequently set the direction for semiconductor manufacturing technologies as other chipmakers subsequently adopted similar approaches. One notable exception was immersion DUV lithography, which Intel adopted after TSMC, which led the development of immersion DUV lithography. By engaging equipment and materials suppliers earlier, Intel could regain some of its historical influence over the development of industry-standard manufacturing solutions, though the importance of this should not be overestimated.

Meanwhile, Intel continues to expand the design ecosystem for its 14A technology. Cadence and Intel have established a design-technology co-optimization (DTCO) collaboration focused on next-generation AI-driven design tools and intellectual property, whereas Keysight, Siemens, and Synopsys have announced design-tool certifications for the process. Intel is also working with other software developers and IP providers to validate design flows and reusable building blocks required by prospective foundry customers.

Intel says it is encouraged by customer feedback regarding the progress of 14A development. However, the company has not identified any customers that have committed to manufacturing products using the technology, disclosed customer tape-outs, or announced specific manufacturing agreements, which is normal for the foundry industry. For Intel Foundry, attracting external customers remains particularly important because development costs of modern leading-edge process technologies can only be recoupled when they are used to make hundreds of millions of chips, way more than Intel sells these days. Meanwhile, the commercial success of Intel Foundry with advanced manufacturing technologies depends not only on transistor performance and manufacturing yields, but also on its ability to deliver predictable development schedules.

Looking ahead, Intel intends to continue improving performance, yields, variability, and manufacturing cycle times of 14A, though for now the company does not disclose any actual goals. Intel also plans additional process enhancements and performance refinements for 14A-E and 14A-P nodes after the initial technology becomes available. However, exact schedules for these nodes are not known at the moment.

"Our next major milestone is the Intel 14A PDK 1.0, expected in the first quarter of 2027," said Ali Farhang, corporate vice president and general manager of Intel Foundry's design technology platform organization. "I am looking forward to sustaining this level of quality across future releases and continuing to earn our customers' trust by delivering on our commitments with a customer-first mindset."

#10390. Google will disable Search for students unless schools accept AI features in ‘Guided Learning’ push

Google is today launching new AI settings for schools, but with the big catch that Search is fully disabled if AI features are turned off entirely.

In a blog post today, Google says it is “focused on building technology responsibly to help students thrive” and, as such, will tailor its AI efforts to help students. “Guided Learning” in Gemini, Search, and Classroom will include helping students “work through problems without skipping straight to answers.”

…if a student pastes a complex physics problem or asks for help writing a book report in Search, they’ll see prompts to help them think critically and work through the concepts, without going directly to the answer.

That’s launching in a pilot program this month in select US schools, with Guided Learning in Google Classroom launching globally today. Google describes this as a “1-1 study tool, anchored in class material to help [students] stay on task” while also giving the teacher insights into how their class is handling the material.

Beyond this new Guided Learning push, Google also says that it will now turn off “Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search” for K-12 student accounts by default “until school administrators explicitly turn them on.”

As The New York Times points out, having those features fully off has a big side effect: it cuts students off from Google Search entirely.

Google says that this is because Search and AI “are deeply integrated” and confirms that students in schools with AI features turned off (which, to reiterate, is now the default setting) “will not have access to search.”

As a reminder, Google Search’s AI features can largely be turned off using a simple modifier.

Google’s post says that the “goal is simple: provide school leaders the control and confidence to use AI products in classrooms as they see fit.” Yet, speaking to The New York Times, Google further explained that two options will be available to schools in “early 2027,” one that has AI Overviews and other AI features turned on, the other with Guided Learning in their place, leaving schools with no choice other than to accept AI features or give up access to Google Search for their students entirely.

NYT points out repeatedly that Google’s prior AI pushes in schools have been met with fierce opposition from educators and parents alike.

#10389. OpenAI introduces program to reduce AI data center energy demand during peak hours

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

This move could help data centers operate sooner without waiting for the rest of the grid to catch up to its peak demand.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Electricity consumption is one of the biggest concerns that residents bring up whenever a data center is proposed in their community, which is why OpenAI is taking steps to minimize its impact on the local electricity grid. According to the company, its Grid Protectors Program aims to reduce the power consumption of its data centers as local demand increases, ensuring that the grid is not overwhelmed during peak hours. Aside from ensuring that the lights remain on for everyone, it would also allow its data centers to connect to the grid and go online sooner instead of waiting for the additional power sources to come online.

“Whether it’s heating homes during a northern cold snap or running air conditioning through a southern heat wave, families need reliable electricity. Our goal is to make campus flexibility a resource that utilities can call on when communities need it most,” OpenAI said in its blog. It also added, “Through Grid Protectors, our ambition is to make this kind of flexibility a core part of how we build and operate future campuses. That means designing facilities to reduce electricity use when needed and working with utilities to agree on how much demand can be reduced, how quickly and for how long. The arrangements will differ by location, and we will share additional commitments as they are finalized.”

OpenAI is testing the program in its planned Effingham County campus in Georgia, with the company saying that it has already signed a 3.2-gigawatt agreement with Georgia Power that included 1,000 megawatts of flexible demand response. This ensures that if demand jumps on the hottest days of the year, there would be enough power to accommodate the hundreds of thousands of air conditioners keeping Georgians cool. With the average American home drawing around 1,200 kilowatts, the 1,000-megawatt buffer is more than enough to power around 800,000 households — nearly 20% of the state’s homes.

This technology, called demand response, has been around for decades now, where large operators reduce their demand in response to a peak in demand from other consumers. Before automation, grid operators would manually call up industrial customers like steel mills and ask them to cut consumption to ensure the reliability of the entire grid. But with modern technologies and IoT, it can now be done automatically, allowing data centers to reduce their load in under a minute. In fact, Nvidia has already been experimenting with a similar system in the UK and has proven that it works.

#10386. ChatGPT has hit roughly 20% of Google Search volume, OpenAI says

According to OpenAI, ChatGPT has managed to grow its search use to around 20% of Google Search’s total volume, a staggering figure.

Google Search has had a massively dominant lead in the search space, with Microsoft’s Bing a distant competitor with a single-digit share at any given time. But, following its push into AI-powered search about 2 years ago, OpenAI says that ChatGPT is catching up in a big way.

Speaking at an OpenAI press event/chat yesterday, ChatGPT consumer product lead Adam Fry confirmed that search volume in ChatGPT is “roughly one-fifth (20%) of the volume of queries of Google.”

There’s a lot of wiggle room there, as the source of Google’s search volume wasn’t revealed. OpenAI’s measurement of what a search query is boils down to when a user inputs a prompt/query that results in a web search – “Every time you ask a question and go search the web, that’s a search.” Again, a lot of wiggle room here.

But, in any case, this would be the biggest rival Google Search has had yet, and certainly explains Google’s massive push to expand AI in Search. Google admits it is facing “lower monetization” with that push, something OpenAI is also surely dealing with in its own efforts. The Verge notes that Fry said that “most people do not seem to mind ads” in ChatGPT and that “we could be doing this more, actually.”

Fry also said that “people really love” ChatGPT’s personalization and understanding.

#10384. Peak Design’s new wireless charging stand pulls double duty, literally

The three new charging accessories from Peak Design hit the high notes, like (dual) Qi2 wireless charging and wired fast charging without bulky bricks.

Last year at CES, Peak Design gave us a sneak preview of its upcoming accessory lineup. Obviously, the company is known for its premium travel bags and accessories, and they make some awesome gear. But they have mobile accessories, too, and the latest release looks pretty cool.

All three new products are charging accessories that work with Android. More specifically, they support the right protocols, like Qi2 25W wireless charging and 65W fast wired charging.

In fact, the 2-in-1 Mobile Charging Station supports dual, simultaneous Qi2 charging. In essence, it’s an adjustable charging stand that either lays flat or props up for a more traditional experience. Premium build materials like aluminum are used for the base, top cradle, and stem. There’s a recessed USB-C port for the adapter you’ll need to source yourself, and a secondary USB-C output port for charging a third device.

To get full power from the 2-in-1 Mobile Charging Station, you’ll need at minimum a 75W charging adapter and cable. The box includes a 100W cable, but charging two devices at 25W wirelessly takes a lot of power. Still, it’s pretty cool that it packs two Qi2 pads, not just one paired with a slower coil.

If that’s not your use case, a 65W charger is enough to power one pad at the full 25W. The absolute minimum required power to charge anything is 45W, Peak Design says on its website.

That might be why Peak Design also launched a slim 65W travel charger. It’s a fantastic little charger that slips into a tech pouch or backpack without much trouble. I carry a slim 65W charger wherever I go, and this easily fits the bill for portability.

It has two USB-C ports. Only one is rated for 65W, and if both are in use, you’re either getting 30W/30W or 45W/20W output. Still, I don’t demand much from a travel charger, and 65W is plenty fast enough to charge a MacBook Air or Pixel 11 within the hour.

The other option is a slim battery. Peak Design also launched a Qi2 Kickstand Travel Battery, capable of 25W wireless fast charging as well. It’s rated for 5,000mAh. Because of the kickstand that wraps around and acts as a frame for the charger, it isn’t as slim as some of the batteries we’re seeing recently. Still, I wouldn’t call it “thick” by any means.

It’s solidly built and has a really nice faux leather finish on both sides, accented by status LEDs on the bottom edge. It also sits really nicely on the Pixel 11 I used it on, which is a point of failure for some. This one feels really nice to use.

Peak Design’s new charging accessories don’t come cheap. The new travel 2-in-1 charging stand is available for $99.95, but that’s really not that bad if you value dual fast wireless charging or have a secondary device or partner to use it with. The battery comes at the same price of $99.95, and the slim 65W charger is available for $64.95.

#10382. Windows 11 8GB RAM configurations get a performance boost

Microsoft's claimed performance gains will need to be tested

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Following a resurgence of 8GB RAM laptops earlier this year, and in the wake of promises to improve Windows 11 performance using just 8GB of RAM, Microsoft has today announced what it claims are a series of performance improvements. Driven by an ongoing global PC component shortage that is forcing manufacturers to dumb down specs, the company says that with 8GB of RAM, Windows is now 20% more responsive and 40% faster when it comes to multitasking.

The development shared today by Microsoft is an update to a commitment earlier this year to improve the quality and performance of Windows 11, a major bugbear of users since its launch. Microsoft says it has taken a three-pronged approach to memory optimization: making better use of memory in Windows 11 PCs, protecting interactions that need to feel instant, and ensuring key apps and Windows use less memory. While the company admits you'll be able to do more on 16GB or 32GB of RAM, it says "we're making sure you can do more, even with 8GB."

The headline performance gain is a claim that Windows is now up to 20% more responsive on 8GB PCs. Likewise, multitasking is now purportedly 40% faster in Microsoft's lab tests. Of course, these are internal tests conducted in favorable conditions by Microsoft.

Microsoft says it has tuned compression for useful apps and data, and can now keep up to 2GB more of your active data in memory, rather than moving it to storage. In practice, this should mean that apps and tabs left open should be less likely to reload when you go back to them.

The claim of 20% more responsiveness specifically relates to tasks like opening Start, searching, right-clicking, opening Settings, using the Snipping Tool, and opening File Explorer. The company says it has achieved this by backing off background tasks like indexing while you're using your PC actively. Edge now reportedly opens up to 33% faster. There's also an Efficiency Mode for Microsoft Teams, which can be set to on by default, improving launch times by 20%.

Microsoft says that its claims of 40% speed boosts to multitasking are based on a demanding workflow of 20 Microsoft Edge Tabs, a 4K video, large Word, PowerPoint, and Excel files, Outlook, and File Explorer all being opened one after the other.

Other improvements include reducing committed-memory growth by up to 900 MB in apps like Microsoft Edge and 365 apps, and reducing the memory footprint of Word, PowerPoint, Excel, and Outlook by 360MB. Third-party apps can also benefit from improved memory management, with Microsoft stating WhatsApp is already taking advantage.

Moving forward, Microsoft says it has plans to make memory compression even smarter and more efficient. Windows features and apps will share common parts, such as Search and Widgets using one web Engine, for instance. WinUI 3 apps like File Explorer and Notepad will be designed to use less memory and open faster, and Microsoft is working with PC makers and silicon manufacturers to reduce how much memory a PC needs to hold in reserve to support its hardware, making more space for Windows.

#10378. How to Optimize AI-Generated Images for Faster Websites

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI-generated images are becoming a common part of modern web development. Developers use them for website banners, product illustrations, landing pages, blog posts, and digital interfaces. However, generating an image is only one part of the process. Before publishing it, developers need to ensure that the image loads quickly, displays correctly across devices, and maintains its visual quality.

Large image files can increase page size, consume unnecessary bandwidth, and delay the appearance of important content. These problems can affect user experience and website performance, particularly on mobile devices and slower internet connections.

A reliable image optimization workflow helps address these challenges. By selecting appropriate dimensions, choosing efficient image formats, automating image processing, and implementing responsive delivery, developers can improve loading performance without sacrificing visual quality.

This guide explains how to optimize AI-generated images using practical techniques and development tools.

Images often account for a significant portion of a webpage's total download size. Uploading large, unoptimized images can make pages slower and increase the amount of data visitors need to download.

Several common problems contribute to poor image performance:

Incorrect dimensions waste bandwidth on smaller screens.

Missing image dimensions can cause unexpected layout shifts.

Poor responsive configuration can make browsers download unnecessarily large images.

Incorrect loading priorities can delay important visual content.

These issues can affect Core Web Vitals, including Largest Contentful Paint (LCP), which measures how quickly the largest visible content element appears.

For developers, image optimization should therefore be part of the website development workflow rather than a final task performed immediately before publication.

Before optimizing an image, determine where it will appear on the website. A full-width banner requires different dimensions from a product thumbnail or a small interface graphic.

For example, a website might use the following dimensions:

These dimensions are examples rather than fixed requirements. The appropriate size depends on the website layout, responsive breakpoints, and supported devices.

When preparing source visuals, developers can explore different AI image generation options and consider how the generated images will fit their intended layouts.

Once the image is ready, keep a high-quality master file. This allows developers to create smaller versions for different screen sizes without repeatedly compressing an already optimized image.

Avoid enlarging small images unnecessarily because upscaling can introduce blur and visible artifacts.

Choosing the right format can reduce image size while preserving acceptable visual quality.

Three commonly used formats are AVIF, WebP, and JPEG.

AVIF: Often provides efficient compression for photographs and detailed illustrations. It can significantly reduce file sizes, although encoding may take longer.

WebP: Supports lossy and lossless compression, transparency, and animation. It offers broad browser support and is suitable for many website images.

JPEG: Remains useful for photographs and situations where compatibility with older software or systems is important.

PNG is appropriate for certain lossless graphics, screenshots, and images that require transparency. However, it can produce unnecessarily large files when used for photographic content.

No format is automatically the best choice for every image. Developers should compare file sizes and inspect visual quality before selecting the final output.

A practical approach is to generate AVIF and WebP versions and provide a JPEG fallback where necessary.

Manually resizing and converting images becomes time-consuming when a website contains dozens or hundreds of visual assets.

Automation makes the process more consistent. Developers can use Sharp, a Node.js image-processing library, to resize images, convert formats, and generate multiple versions from a single source file.

Next, create a file named build-images.mjs:

The script creates multiple image sizes in both WebP and AVIF formats.

The quality settings are starting points, not universal recommendations. Developers should adjust them after checking the resulting file sizes and visual details.

This approach is particularly useful for content-heavy websites, product catalogs, and applications that publish images regularly.

Responsive image delivery allows browsers to select an appropriate image based on the visitor's screen and the space available in the layout.

HTML provides two important attributes for this purpose: srcset and sizes.

The srcset attribute lists the available image files and their widths. The sizes attribute describes how wide the image is expected to appear at different viewport sizes.

In this example, the image is expected to occupy approximately 720 pixels on wider screens and nearly the full viewport width on smaller screens.

The browser uses this information to choose an appropriate image candidate.

One common mistake is setting sizes="100vw" for an image that occupies only a small part of the page. This can encourage the browser to download a larger file than necessary.

Always ensure that the sizes attribute reflects the actual website layout.

The HTML picture element allows developers to provide multiple image formats and a fallback option.

The browser selects a supported format, while the img element provides the fallback image and alternative text.

Developers should verify that every referenced file exists and that its dimensions match the intended layout.

The picture element can also support different crops for desktop and mobile devices. This is useful when a wide banner needs a separate composition for smaller screens.

Images without defined dimensions can cause content to move while the browser loads the file. This behavior contributes to Cumulative Layout Shift (CLS).

Including width and height attributes helps browsers reserve the appropriate space before the image finishes loading.

For responsive layouts, developers can use CSS:

This allows images to scale within their containers while maintaining their proportions.

Images below the initial viewport can generally use native lazy loading:

However, the main hero image should not normally be lazy-loaded when it is responsible for LCP. Delaying an important above-the-fold image can make the page appear slower.

Where appropriate, developers can use fetchpriority="high" for the main image to help the browser prioritize its download.

The correct strategy depends on the image's position and importance within the page.

Image optimization involves more than file size and dimensions. Developers should also consider accessibility, file organization, and metadata.

Alternative text helps people using screen readers understand meaningful images. It should describe the image's purpose in the context of the page.

Decorative images that provide no useful information should generally use an empty alt attribute.

File names should also be descriptive. Names such as home-office-sunrise.webp are easier to understand and maintain than generic names such as image-final-v3.png.

Metadata requires additional consideration. Image-processing tools may remove metadata by default to reduce file size. This can include information that a project needs to preserve, depending on its requirements.

Before removing metadata, developers should review whether the website or client requires attribution, provenance information, or other embedded details.

After implementing an optimization workflow, test the actual website rather than relying only on exported file sizes.

Chrome DevTools provides useful information about image requests.

Open Developer Tools and select the Network panel.

Resize the viewport or use device emulation.

Check whether the browser downloads appropriately sized images.

Developers can also inspect the image selected by the browser through the console:

This returns the current image URL selected for that element.

For websites with multiple images, inspect the relevant elements individually.

Finally, run an audit using Lighthouse or PageSpeed Insights. These tools can identify issues involving oversized images, image formats, loading priorities, and other performance factors.

Image optimization is only one part of website performance. Server response times, caching, CSS, JavaScript, and third-party scripts can also influence loading speed.

Several mistakes can reduce the effectiveness of an image optimization workflow:

Uploading original images without checking their dimensions.

Assuming one format always produces the smallest file.

Omitting the sizes attribute from responsive images.

Lazy-loading important above-the-fold images.

Applying excessive compression and damaging visual details.

Removing metadata without checking project requirements.

Measuring file size without inspecting the final visual quality.

A consistent testing process helps identify these issues before they affect visitors.

No. AVIF often provides efficient compression, but the results depend on the image, encoding settings, and quality requirements. Comparing both formats is the best way to choose an appropriate option.

Four to six sizes may be sufficient for many websites. The ideal number depends on the layout, responsive breakpoints, and supported devices.

Yes. Reducing the size of the main content image, providing responsive versions, and avoiding unnecessary loading delays can improve LCP when that image is the largest contentful element.

No. Lazy loading is useful for images outside the initial viewport. Important images near the top of the page should generally load promptly.

The fundamental techniques are the same as those used for conventional images. However, AI-generated visuals may contain complex textures, gradients, and fine details that require careful inspection after compression.

AI-generated images can improve website design and make it easier to create custom visual content. However, efficient delivery requires more than exporting an image and uploading it to a website.

Developers should choose suitable dimensions, compare modern image formats, automate processing, and implement responsive image delivery. Including image dimensions helps prevent layout shifts, while appropriate loading priorities can improve the experience for visitors.

By testing images across different devices and reviewing their actual performance, development teams can maintain visual quality while reducing unnecessary downloads.

A repeatable optimization workflow makes it easier to publish new visuals without sacrificing website speed, accessibility, or reliability.

#10372. How to Test AI-Generated Content Before It Reaches Your Users

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most teams now ship something an AI helped write: help-center articles, product descriptions, release notes, even code comments. The draft appears in seconds, and that speed is the trap. Nobody tests it the way they would test a new feature, so mistakes slip through that a quick review would have caught.

This guide is about the other side of AI testing. Instead of using AI to test your software, you test what AI hands you. Here is a simple routine that fits into a normal publishing or release process.

AI writing sounds confident whether or not it is right. A model can invent a statistic (researchers call this hallucination and have documented it across many text-generation systems), quote a feature your product does not have, or describe a setting that was renamed two releases ago. The sentence reads smoothly, so the error hides in plain sight.

Traditional QA does not cover this. A unit test tells you the page loads. It says nothing about whether the claim on the page is true. That gap is where most AI-related incidents begin.

1. Define what "good" means before you generate anything. Write down the audience, the claims you are willing to make and the sources you trust. A short brief makes the output easier to judge, because you are comparing it with something concrete.

2. Check every factual claim. Highlight each number, name and statement of fact, then verify it against a primary source. Google's own guidance on generative AI content tells publishers to fact-check AI output, including titles, descriptions and alt text, before publishing. If you cannot confirm a claim in a minute or two, cut it or rewrite it as something you can support.

3. Test the instructions. For tutorials and how-to content, follow the steps exactly as written on a clean machine or account. Commands that fail and menus that no longer exist show up immediately this way.

4. Review the voice. Replace vague phrases with specifics from your own experience: a real customer question, a real error message, a real number from your analytics. This is also what separates useful content from filler.

5. Keep a human owner. Every piece should have a named person who approved it. The NIST AI Risk Management Framework makes the same point at organization level: people stay accountable for how AI is used and evaluated. If something goes wrong later, you know who to ask and where to fix it.

Some teams also run drafts through an AI detector. That can be useful as a prompt for a closer look, for example when freelancers or vendors deliver work and you want to know how much of it was machine-generated. A high score is a reason to read more carefully. It is not proof of anything, and it should never decide an outcome on its own, because detectors make mistakes in both directions. A Stanford study found that GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers, and short texts are easy to misjudge too. If you want a quick second opinion on a draft, a tool like Tester AI can give you a starting point for that conversation.

Review works best when it is not optional. A few small habits make it stick.

Treating the first draft as finished. It is a starting point. Plan time for review the same way you plan time for writing.

Reviewing for grammar only. Clean sentences are not evidence of correct content. Check meaning first and polish second.

Letting the tool grade its own work. Asking the same model to confirm its answer rarely catches real errors. Use a source or a person.

Skipping legal and brand review. Generated text can echo existing material or make promises your company cannot keep. Anything customer-facing deserves a second pair of eyes.

AI can save hours of drafting, but speed only helps if the result is trustworthy. Treat generated content like code from a new contributor: useful, often good, and always reviewed before it goes live. A short routine of defining quality, verifying facts, testing instructions and keeping a human owner will catch most problems before your users do.

Do I need to test AI-generated content if a human edits it? Yes. Editing for style does not confirm that the facts are correct. Verify claims separately from polishing the wording.

How long should a review take? For a typical article, expect roughly the time it takes to read it twice plus a few minutes per factual claim. Reviews are faster when the brief and sources are prepared in advance.

Can an AI detector prove that text was written by a machine? No. Detectors estimate likelihood, and they can be wrong. Use the result as a signal for human review, never as the final decision.

What should I check first? Start with numbers, names, dates and anything a customer could act on, such as prices, steps or legal statements. Those carry the highest risk if they are wrong.
